2 MCDoTTG A [A,s PATENT SELF-RAISING FLOUR MAKES THE BEST PASTRY.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS,

WITH the average playwright it is all work and no play. A TALE well told is twice told, but a tale twic% told becomes monotonous.

OF all sorts of embarrassment, that of riches is the most easily overcome.

THE man who always looks before he leaps, never leaps.

KEEP your troubles to yourself; when you tell them you are taking up the time of the man who is waiting to tell you his. IF we could live our lives again, we would no doubt avoid a great many of our mistakes and renlace them by an entirely different assortment. IT is a slow baby that does not know which of its relatives is most easily imposed upon.

GETTING THE BETTER OF ROBERT.—One of the .better order of " bikists " had ventured on the footpath for a short distance—this was in Surrey —to avoid a newlv-macadamised bit of road. Up popped a man in blue. "'Your name and address, please.'' Cyclist gave card containinc, his right name and address. Constable (reading): "'S. J. Smith, 2, Raspberry Crescent, 'Arrow-on-the-'lll.' Go on with you.! I want your proper name and address; we've too many Smiths about here, or I'll run you in." Cyclist (desperately) : "Then, if you must have it, it's William Shakespeare, Stratford-on-Avon." Constable : "Thank you, sir; sorry to have troublsd you." (Proceeds to enter the name and address carefully in his book.) MRS. Trott; "Oh, I 'only went into town on a shopping expedition ! " Mrs. Streete : "Did you? Tell me all about it. Did you have a real good time ? " Mrs. Trott, "Not at all. I found what I wanted in the very first place I went into." Mrs. Streete : "How provoking ! "

FAIR MAIDEN (at the football game, during an exciting mele6) : "Oh, look there—can't Jack- hug just beautifully!" SUGGESTED A SOLUTION.—An ostentatious mem- ber of a certain County Council, whose father is well-known as a retired omnibus-driver, was one day displaying a large seal he usually wears, repre- senting St. George and the Dragon, and while several bystanders were expressing their admira tion of it, its owner remarked, in solemn tones— " Aw, one of my ancestors is—aw—supposed to have killed the dwagon—aw.--don't you know." "Dear me," inquired one of his hearers, who knew something about him, "did he run over it?" ADAM'S NURSE.—A Sunday-school superinten- dent at the close of an address on the Creation, which he felt sure he had kept within the compre- hension of the least scholars, smilingly invited questions. A tiny boy with a white, eager face and large brow, at once held up his hand. "Please, sir, why was Adam never a baby?" The superintendent coughed in some doubt as to what answer to give, but a little girl of nine, the eldest of several brothers and sisters, came promptly to his aid. "Please, sir," she said, smartly, " there was nobody to nuss him ! " CHOLLY Fiveclock ; " Yaas, I twied to learn to play golf last summah, don't y' know, but I gave it up when I got struck on the head with the ball and knocked silly." Miss Quickwit "How sad! And can the doctors do nothing for you "

NOT LIKE THAT. —Here is a story illustrative of the prosaic nature on which art makes no im- pression :—ln Westminster Abbey there is a large marble tablet in memory of a famous bishop. It is a basrelief, representing the bishop—a portrait— in the agony of death, sinking into the arms of an allegorical female figure, presumably intended for the Angel of Death. It is said that an aged couple from the country were being shown round the Abbey, and that, pausing long before the tablet, the old lady remarked to her husband—" That's a good likeness of the bishop, but" (regarding the angelic personage attentively) "it's a very poor one of Mrs. —. I knew her well, and she didn't look like that !"

SMART.—A north-countryman went to a solicitor, laid before him a case in dispute, and then asked him if he would undertake- to win the suit. "Certainly," replied the solicitor, "I will readily undestake the case.. We are sure to win !" "So you really think it is a good case ?" "Un- doubtedly, my dear sir. I am prepared to guarantee you will secure a verdict in your favour." "Very good, sir; I'm much obliged to you, but I slon't think I'll go in for law this time; for, you see, the case I have laid before you is my opponent's ! " WILLING TO ,EXCHANGE.—The pastor bade her proceed.—" Tell me all," he urged, kindly, "I put a button in the contribution box" she faltered. He smiled. "And did your conscience trouble you ? " he asked. The woman raised her eyes earnestly. "No," she answered. "I put in the wrong button and broke a set, and I would like to exchange it, if you please." AT LAST.—The stout party with the eye-glasses gazed with rapture at the rolling, dark-blue waves. "At last," he said, " the dream of my life is realised? I have the glories of the mighty deep before my eyes, the moisture of the salt breeze for the first time on my cheek !" Here he wept. The careless loungers of the sandy shore', recognising the celebrated writer of sea stories, respected his emotion, and stood silent, nor jarred upon his joy with their idle babble.

DIFFERENT.—They were talking about how they happened to marry. "I married- my wife," said one, after the others had all had their say; "be- -cause she was different from any woman I had ever met." "How was that?" chorused the others. "She was the only woman I ever met who would lave me."

PRECEPT AND EXAMPLE. —Scene: A Sunday School. Johnnie: "I say, Miss Jones, I know now why you dial'nt want me to rob birds' nests last, spring." Miss Jones (with an oriole in her hat) : " Why was it Johnnie V' Johnnie (gazing with admiring eyes at the hat) : "'Cause you wanted the birds to grow big enough to wear 'em." A CRISIS.—Under the electric lights the whole room was seen to be in a state of wild disorder. Articles of vertu were strewn over the carpet, mingled with many toilet accessories. Bureau drawers and cupboards were pulled open and their contents hung out. The furniture was dragged out of place and upset, and rugs lay in crumpled heaps. In the midst of this terrible disarray two human beino.s confronted each other; one a woman, the other a mocking, jeering fiend whom the law called her husband. He eves had a strange, hunted look ; her nervous hands pressed hard on her feverish brow. She strove for speech, but it was denied her, while the man still laughed his cruel, triumph- ant laugh. At last, with a stifled groan, she fell swooning on the floor, irrevocably disarranging her stiff shirt-front. She was a new woman, she had lost her collar-button, and she did not know how to swear. TRUE TEST OF NERVE.—" I used to think," said Mr. Gratebar, "when I read of generals calmly dictating despatches amid the carnage and uproar of the battle, what nerve " But now as I try to write a letter here at home, with the two older children in the parlom- playing on the piano, and 'singing with the vigour and voice of youth, the two younger children in the dining-room learning their lessons for the morrow, One times one is one; two times two is two ; three times three is three,' and Did the cat catch the rat? No, the cat did not catch the rat. Why did not the cat catch the rat?" with a carpet-sweeper obbligato by Mrs. Gratebar, and an occasional variation by the two younger children racing through the hall, coupled with a grand instrumental and staccato in the parlour, why I say to myself, No, no; the true test or nerve conies not in the stormier scenes of life, but amid the delightful repose of home.'" NECESSARY PRECAUTION. —A good story is told in connection with the final for the English Cup, decided not long back at the Crystal Palace. Prior to the match, it will be remembered, the Aston Villa and West Bromwich Albion teams were photographed, a proceeding by no means unusual. Among the crowd was a countryman who, apparently, had taken advantage of the cheap -excursion more with a view of exploring the Palace than of witnessing the match. " Wot be they a- takin' theer fort;,,graffs for, I wonder?" he ex- claimed to his neighbour, a youth from Birming- ham. The latter smiied at the ignorance of his questioner, and replied, with the air of one who knows what he is talking about,— "That is a necessary precaution." " Oh, aye," said the countryman, as wise as ever. "You see," added the yokh, "if they didn't take their photographs the committees would never be able to, sort out their own men after a scrummage."

"CAPITAL" CHAT.

Some interesting particulars ht,ve been given b:,. a Russian workman fresh from the great strike in Russia, which has attracted so much notice. Of the 30,000 persons on strike, 10,000 are women, and the majority of the 30,000 are textile workers. The " casus belli which produced the strike lay in the circumstances connected with the Czar's corona- tion. The police ordered the workpeople to take three days' holiday, for which the employers stopped the three days' pay. Their workpeople in- sisted upon payment, and when the masters held out they struck work. But the strike immediately broadened ont into other issues, the principal being the reduction of the daily hours of work from 15 to U. For this slavery the men are paid from 15s. to 20s. per week, and the women 108. 61. But what a life of drudgery and starvation. Hours so atrocious and wages so pitifully small justify something more than mere discontent; but 1 understand that in Russia one of the penalties of organising a strike as a protest against so inhuman a lot is a compulsory and protracted visit to Siberia.

I have just seen a private circular issued by the directors of a powerful and highly connected Rho- desian company to their shareholders, stating that they have been assured on the highest authority that it will be utterly impossible to send out from any of the large centres of population a single pros- pector or mining agent for the next twelve months. if they find any white man ta go into the isolated districts to which a man engaged in prospecting operations must go, they will not take the responsi- bility of sending him in face of the chance that he will fall a victim to some band of natives and never return. 1 his circular, I have no doubt, expresses the general feeling of other Rhodesian companies. Another company has also just sent in a large claim against the Chartered Company for the outlay it has incurred in despatching a body of young engineers ptospectors, and clerks to Rhodesia. keeping them there for some time, and finally sending them home again in consequence of the hopeless condition of the country as regards mining work. tither com- panies, I believe, will follow with similar claims. All this is significant of the view taken by men of shrewdness and Experience on the spot, and the facts I have mentioned point to a long and tedious intervtil before the territo:y is restored to a healthy administrative condition.

Nothing is yet known with regard to the con- stitution of the Commission on Old-age Pensions. But there is remarkably iittle curiosity on the sub- ject in the House of Commons. The belief, indeed, appears to be almost universal that the inquiries ot ti e Commission will come to nothing, and it is well ki.own that several leading members of the Govern- ment would be very sorry if they promised to have any result. Sir M. Hicks-Beach, for instance, is credited with the view that any working scheme of old-81.-,,e pensions is impracticable ; and there are certainly a large number of the rank and file of the Unionist party who are prepar,,d to give their opin- ion to any who may ask for it that no bill can possibly be devised which will pass the House of Commons. however, Mr. Chamberlain appears to have insisted on something being done, and he has attained his end. The appointment of the Commis- sion will at least do something to shield the Secre- tary for the Colonies from awkward inquiries dur- ing the recess.

I learn that the leading Liberals in Quebec con- template sending a special delegate to Rome to procure a decision from the Vatican with regard to the exercise of Roman Catholic clerical influence in Canadian political affairs. The friends of Mr. Laurier declare that the conduct of Monsignor lif,fn2he and others of the Roman clergy during the recent electoral campaign renders an appeal to Rome a matter of necessity.

Sir William Harcourt is more popular at present with his party than he wo..s ever before. He stayed in the House on Tuesday morning till half-past eight o'clock, and it is noteworthy that Lady Har- court was a spectator in the ladies' gallery during the hours of the early morning. Lady Frances Bal- four was also in the gallery during the greater por- tion of the night.

The East End is far* from being the only home of the London costermonger. In the district imme- diately south of the Thames his presence is a necessity, inasmuch as he brings to the doors of a teeming population various supplies at a very low price. Next in importance to the street trader himself is the long-suffering animal who accom- panies him in his daily round, and to say that the costermonger seldom fails to treat his coadjutor fairly and even handsomely is to do him no more than justice. At Herne Hill on Tuesday afternoon ocular demonstration was given of these happy relations between master and servant in the sleek and well-fed appearance of 127 donkeys which coirpeted for prizes at a show held in the London County Athletic Grounds. Lord Sudley and his fellow-judgfs had no easy task before them in apportioning rewards, and the successful claimants for the prize of five shillings given for good groom- ing proved to be very numerous. Early in the evening the Duke alighted at the pavilion, where, after due words of commendation had been spoken, the proud costers received the promised coins at the hands of the Queens granddaughter.

The agenda paper for the coming International Labour Congress is a very interestiLg study, as it specifically indicates the principles put forward for discussion by a large number of Labour parties throughout Europe. It enables us thus to see what is uppermost in the thoughts of the European 'workers," to adopt the expression that is fashion- able in Labour circles in these later days; and it is. is fact, a chronicle of the doctrines of the school of the most modern economics. Various Dutch Labour Unions want respectively—first, a clear de- finition of what is comprehended within, and ex- cluded from, the term " political action?' which is recommended in many quarters as the best means of emancipating the workers; second. the abandon- ment of the agitation for en eight. haurs' day on the Ist of May, and substitution for it of an agita- tion in favour of the convert ion of the means of pro- duction and consumption from private into collec- tive proprrty ; third, an international strike by all workers in the countries affected,as a protest against any declaration of war by any Governmen!-.; fotirthly,the organisation Of socialist schoolmasters; fifthly, the legislative reonlation of wages and working hours; and, sixthly, the establishment of an eight hours' day. Coming to Poland and Russia, the Labour party in the former country call for the independence and autonomy of Poland. The Russian Labour party in Poland advocate, first the international solidarity of the worker for the sup- pression of the capitalist, ; and, second, that the sup- pression of Russian absolutism is a cause which concerns the whole working classes of Europe.

Capitala's blood seemed to turn to ice, and her form to stone at the sight! Her first impulse waq to scream and let fall the waiter! She controlled herself and repressed the scream, though she was very near dropping the waiter. Black Dzinald looked at her and laughed aloud at her consternation, saying with a chuckle: "You did not expect to see me here to-night, did you now, my dear e She gazed at him in a.silent panic for a moment.

Then- her faculties, that had been suddenly dis- persed by the shock, as suddenly rallied to her rescue.

In one moment .she understood her real position..

Black Donald had locked her in with himself, and held the key; so she could not hope to get out:

The loudest ;Cream that she might: utter- would never reach the distant chamber of Major Warfield, or the still more remote apartment of Mrs. Condi- ment; so she could not hope to bring anyone to her assistance.

She was therefore entirely in the power of Black Donald. She fully comprehended this, and said to herself:

"Now, my dear Cap, if you don't look sharp your hoer is come! Nothing on earth will save you, Cap, but your own wits I for if ever I saw mischief in anyone's face, it is in that fellow's that is eating you up with his great eyes at the same time that he is laughing at you with his big mouth! All stratagems are fair in love and war— especially in war, and most especially in such a war as this is likely to be—a contest in close quarters for dear life I "

All this passed through her miud in one moment, and in the next her plan was formed. Setting her waiter down upon the table, and throwing herself into one of the arm-chairs, she said:

Ali! you little vampire, how you thirsted for my blood! And you pretended to like me 1" said Black Donald, eyeing her from head to foot, with a sly leer. Cap returned the look with interest. Capitola lay upoo the bed, with her Luxe buried in the pillow, the greater portion of the time from two o'clock until day. An uncontrollable horror prevented her from turning lest she should see the yawning mystery in the middle of the floor, or hear some awful sound from its unknown depths. The very shadows on the walls thrown up wildly by the expiring firelight, were objects of grotesque terror. Never, never, in the whole youth of stnnge vicis- situde, had the nerves of this brave girl been so tremendously shaken and prostrat ed. It was late in the morning when at last nature sticcumbed, and she sank into a deep sleep. She had not slept long when she was aroused from a pro- found state of insensibility by a loud, impatient knocking at her door.

. -- She started up wildly and gazed around her. Then the yawning trap-door, the remnants of the supper, and Black Donald's coat, hat and boots upon the floor, drove in upon her reeiing brain the memory of the night of terror! Thus scolding, Old Hurricane reached the spot, and began to ply screw-drivers and chisels until at length the strong lock yielded, and he opened the door. "I drew him to sit upon a chair on the rug, and—" again she shuddered from head to foot—" and I sprung the trap and precipitated him to—oh, Heaven of Heavens 1 where.:? I know not !" "But you—you were unharmed 2" "Yes, yes I" "Oh, Cap! Oh, my dear Car! Thank Heaven for that !"

"But, uncle, where—oh, where did he go 7" in- quired Cl.pitola, almost wildly. "Who the demon cares? To perdition, 'I hope and trust, with all my heart and soul 1" cried Old Hurricane, with emphasis, as he approached and looked down the opening. "Uncle, what is below there ?" asked Capitola, anxiously pointing down the abyss. "An old cellar, as I have told you, and Black Donald, as you have just told me. HILLO THERE! are you killed, as you deserve to be, you atrocious villain ?" roared Old Hurricane, stooping down into the opening. A feeble, distant moan answered him. Oh, heaven ! he is living! he is living! I have not killed him F' cried Capitola, clasping her hands. "Why, I do believe you are glad of it 1" ex- claimed Old Hurricane, in astonishment.

" Oh, yes, yes, yes! for it was a fearful thought that I had been compelled to take a sacred life I " to send an immortal soul unprepared to its account I" _

"Well! his neck isn't broken, it appears, or he couldn't groan; but I hope and trust that every other bone in his body is Mrs. Condiment, mum! I'll trouble you to put on your bonnet and walk over to Ezy's and tell him to come here directly I must send for the constable," said Old Hurricane, going to the door and speaking to his housekeeper, who, with an appalled countenance, had been a silent spectator of all that had passed. As soon as the old woman had gone to do her errand he turned again, and stooping down the hole, exclaimed:

"I say, you scoundrel down there! What do you think of yourself now? Are you much hurt, you knave? Is every one of your bones broken, as they deserve to be, you villain? Answer me, you varlet 1" A low, deep moan was the only response, "If that means yes, I'm glad to hear it, you wretch. You'll go to the camp-meeting with us again, won't you, you knave! You'll preach against evil passions and profane swearing, looking right straight at me all the time, until you bring the eyes of the whole congregation upon me as a sinner above all sinners, you scoundrel 7 You'll turn me out of my own bed and away from my own board, won't you, you villain? Won't you, precious Father Gray? Oh, we'll Father Gray you I Demmy, the next time a trap-door falls under you, you rascal, there shall be a rope round your neck to keep you from the ground, pr cious Father Gray 1" "Uncle! Uncle that is cowardly 1" exclaimed Oaptola. "What is cowardly, Miss Impertinence?"

"To insult and ainit.e a fallen man who is in your power! The poor man is badly hurt, may be dying, for aught you know, and you stand over him and berate him when he cannot even answer you " llmph, umph, umph ; demmy, you're—umph, well, he is fallen, fallen pretty baaly, eh? and if he should come around after this, the next fall he gets will be like to break his neck, eh ?—I say, you gentleman below there Mr. Black Donald precious Father Gray—you'll keep quiet, won't you, while we go and get our hreakfast ? Do, now! Come, Cap, come down and pour out my coffee, and by the time we get through, old Ezy will be here."

- . Cantola complied, and they left the room together. The overseer came in while they were at breakfast, and with his hair standing on end, listened to the account of the capture of the outlaw by our heroine. "And now saddle Fleetfoot and ride for your life to Tip Top and bring a pair of constables," were the last orders of Old Hurricane.

While Mr. Ezy was gone on his errand, Major Warfield, Capitola. and Mrs. Condiment remained below stairs. - -

It was several hours before the messenger returned with the constables, and with several neighbours whom interest and curiosity had insti- gated to join the party. As soon as they arrived, a long ladder was pro- cured and carried up into Capitola's chamber, and let down through the trap door. Fortunately it was long enough, for when the foot of the ladder found the floor of the cellar, the head rested securely against the edge of the opening. In a moment the two constables began singly to descend, the foremost one carrying a lighted candle in his hand.

The remaining members of the party, consisting of Major Warfield, Capitola, Mrs. Condiment, and some half dozm neighbours, remained gathered around the open trap-door, waiting, watching, and listening for what might next happen. Presently one of the constables called oat: "Major Warfield, sir! "Well I " replied Old Hurricane. "He's a-breathing still, sir; but seems badly hurt, and may be a-dying, seeing as he's nnsensible and unspeakable. What shall we do along of him 2"

"Bring him up t let's have a look at the fellow, at any rate I" exclaimed Old Hurricane, peremp- torily. _ _

":Just so, sir but some of the gem-men up there'll have to come down on the ladder and give a lift. He's dead weight now, I tell your honour!"

Several of the neighbours immediatelyvolunteered for the service, and two of the strongest descended the ladder to lend their aid.

On attempting to move the injured man he uttered a cry of pain, and fainted, and then it took the united strength and skill of four strong men to raise the huge insensible form of the athlete, and get him up the ladder. No doubt the motion greatly inflamed his inward wounds, but that could not be helped. They got him up at last, and laid out upon the floor, a ghastly, bleed- ing, insensible form, around which every one gathered to gaze. While they were all looking upon him as upon a slaughtered wild beast, Capi- tola alone felt compassion. "Uncle, he is quite crushed by his fall. Make- the men lay him upon the bed. Never think of me; I shall never occupy this room again;, its associations are too full of horrors. There, uncle, make them at once lay him upon the bed." "I think the young lady is right, unless we mean to let the fellow die," said one of the neighbours. Very well! I have particular reasons of my own for wishing that the man's life should be spared until he could be brought to trial and Induced to give up his accomplices," said Old Hurricane. Then turning to his ward, he said : "Come along, Capital. Mrs, Condiment will see that your effects are transferred to another apartment." "And you, friends.," he continued, addressing the men present, "be so good, so soon as we have gone. as to undress that fellow and put him to bed, and examine his injuries while I send off for a physician ' - for I consider it Ix ry, important that his life should be spared suffiaiently long te enable him to give up his accomplices." And so sayi,g, old Hurricane drew the arm of Capitola within his own and left the room.

It was noon before the physician arrived. When he had examined the patient, he pronounced him utterly unfit to be removed, as besides other serious contusions zind bruises, his legs were broken and several of his ribs fractured.

In a word, it was several weeks before the strong constitution of the outlaw prevailed over his many injuries, and he was pronounced well enough to be taken before a magistrate and committed to prison to wait his trial. Alas I his life, it was said, was forfeit by an hundred crimes, and there could be no doubt as to his fate. He maintained a self- possessed, good-humoured, and laughingly defiant manner, and when asked to give up his accomplices, he answered gaily: "That treachery was a legal virtue which outlaws could not be expected to know anything about." Capitola was everywhere landed for her brave part in the capture of the famous desperado. But Cap was too sincerely sorry for Black Donald to care for the applause. (to be continued).

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

Simple washing frocks, whether all white or in light zephyrs or cambrics, are delightful for children in warm weather, and require no wrap for outdoor wear, but merely a large hat, either of drawn washing silk on a tulle frame, or of Leg- horn, with satin bows and white feathers. The banded waist is now very popular for girls of nine to 15, and is especially becoming to tall, growing girls of 11 to 12, where the more childish Empire style appears unsuitable, and gives additional length to the figure. Spotted muslin, made up on blue sateen, or over a thin white linen, is charm- ing with the bodice trimmed with braces and bands

,of insertion lace. 'For a girl of 11 to 13, "the skirt , should measure three yards at the edge. There is .a whole 'width at back :and front, gored to the top, and one gore on each side, this sloped .on one edge, only leaving the straight -edivedge, to the ,front. The top is kept tolerably :plain in front, but gathered •round the sides and beck, and attached to the bodice. he lining of the bodice is cut in three parts. The material is cut in similar fashion to the lining, but .with about four inches extra allowed in the centre front for fulness, and three inches to each half-back. The lace is laid on afterwarcic, and the waist finished with ribbon. Large hats are very general, and these,are wider Irom side to .side, and rather shallow in 'front, and the back is-invariably turned up and filled in with flowers or ribbon bows. Of the hats in quiet tones, black and white is extremely fashionable, -the shape either in white rice straw, or chip, bound,with tlack velvet, stretched quite tight in the old-fashioned style, or a dead-white fancy -crinolinewith irregular-scalleped edge. •

A ~very pretty hat tri-aimed with ostrich feathers is of fawn straw, lined with black velvet, and trimmed with pink reses, black tips, and shoterose-coloured glace ribbons. Another hat may be trimmed with black and white, with pink roses.. _ _ _

at the back; oraN7laite hat is charming iiith dahlia. or heliotrope ribbon, a black plume, paste buckle,. and band of black velvet round the crown. A. black hat with white edge is 4150 effective, with. shot rote and crimson ribbon, black pluie, and. pink roses ;'and the style of tim trimming can be copied in any of these combinations. .Although the bodiGe is still the most .elaborate: portion of the gown, yet skirts 4composed of very- thin, or !semi-transparent, textures are generally- trimmed:; while silks., satins, and, heavier fabrics are left severely plain. Black silk canvas, .grena- dine, ,or gauze generagy shows one to three,smalt ,pleated flounces, .headed by insertion, or full- „gathered frills headed by a ruche, c.r tiny frills of lace, with .chiffon or gauze rosettes at iiateals, ;are seen ou.the plainer type, of material.

- 1 have seen some very pretty bodices this week -of pointed fouiard in rich Oriental colours, with the new sleeves with three puff?. At the neck is shown the .chemisette and jabot of lace with blue eatin ribbon colla.r and bows at the neck and waist. With summer he3t upon us at last, ano in the superlative degree, cottons are for the moLicnt th'e most interesting dress fabrics, and next in_poptr- larity comes the cool and dust-resisting alpaca. I have a particular fancy -for steel-grey alpaca, made up with white, 13ut the right tint is not obtainable in the cheaper make, which is chiefly represented in shot effects. I saw a charming gown of this description yesterday. It was- a coat -and skirt, the former open-fronted, and very simply en with, large pointed revers carefully lined and boxed, to obviate limpness. The full vest, with folded waist, belt, shaped to a slight point in front, was of ivory 14Tagpore silk, and a tucker and cravat of lace was worn at the neck. The hat was of white rush, trimmed with white tulle and pale pink ca,rna. tions, and with white kid gloves, the wearer looked the personification of coolness and elegance, and yet the whole toilette was simplicity itself. Drill and linen gowns are always nice for summer wear, though they are not exactly cheap, for the material must be of fair quality, or it will not wash satis- factorily. However, a nice drill or linen costume, if made in a style that does not show date too much, will wear for at least two, and perhaps three successive summers.

Your loving JENNIE.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

The State Concert held at Buckingham Palace on Monday last, was largely attended, and lacked but the presence of the Queen to be a gathering of quite un- usual brilliancy. e The jewels and gowns worn by the women, and th gay uniforms and orders donned by the men made a gorges picture, and never were flowers so Many of my readers may be interested tmoultenhowin ue that these ese concerts take place in the magnifi- cent ball room, a stage being erected for the performers. The Royalties eat iu front on scarlet and gold chairs,and

the order of precedence is strictly, observed when ths Princes and Princesses take their places. The guests are accommodated upon benches arranged down the room (which by the way are neither very handsome nor earticularly comfortable) and they are all seated before the Royal procession enters headed by the great officers of the Household. The gold plate which is sent up from Windeor Castle for the State concerts, amounts to about 10,000 pieces, and is carried by special train under escort of a guard of soldiers and delivered to the butler at Buckingham Palace Be gives a receipt and is responsible for it while it remains at the -Palace. The same formalities are observed in taking it back and all persons concerned ar3 considerably relieved when it is once more restored to the safe keeping of the gold pantry. INi-ten the Queen is in residence either at Windsor or Osborne, all toe Royal linen is washed at Richmoud. DIE RECENT TRIPLE EXECUTION.

ALLEGED HORRIBLE SCENE.

"'Lloyd's News" asserts that,despite the official state- ment that the triple execution of Fowler, Seaman, and Milsom passes off in a satisfactory manner, there was considerable bungling. "The execution," i; adds, "was carried out in the presence of Under-Sheriff Met- ' mile, acting for the High Sheriff of the County of Lon- don, Under-Sheriff Rushton, and certain gaol officials. Sillington was the hangman, having as his assistant Wilkinson. of Bolton. The cells of the prisoners are but a few yards from the execction shed, and in the order of things, Fowler was the first to undergo the process of pinioning, and to be placed upon the drop, Seaman coming next, and Milsom last. The chaplain, reading the Burial Service, reached the solemn words, " Inthe midst of life we are in death" at the exact moment when the assistant was strapping the legs-of Milsom. The words quoted are usually the notification to the sheriffs-for the signal for death, and ignorant or careless of the fact that the preparations were not quite complete, they gave it. Wilkinson's hands were still busy with the straps about Maori's feet as Billington pulled‘the lever, and the convict and the assistant-hangman 'fell through the trap together. Then ensued a horrible scene, Shooting head foremost down, Wilkinson, to save his own neck from the full depth of the pit, overtwenty feet, clutched to the legs of the unhappy culprit, and swung there with him. Thectoeping attitude of the assistant naturally threw him forward as he clung to Milsom's legs, so that the 4etter did not get a direct drop, and the first impact of the noose came upon the babk of the neck in- stead of slightly forward as it should do, to ensure in- stantaneous death. Milsom was duly hanged, but it was a death of minutes, not seconds, as in the case of his companions."

The " Sun " avers, on what is stated to be indis- putable.authoritv, that what really occurred was that Milsom fell into the -pit with his legs unstrapped, and thnt Wilkinson did not fall into the pit, hut got down and held Milsom's legs. Fowler and Seaman, it is aided, died instantly and painlessly,but Milsom's agony was frightfully prolonged, and death was due to suffo- cation rather than to dislocation of thovetebras.

BILLMGTON INTERVIEWED,

'BilFnaton was interviewed on Monday with reference to the statPment in "Lloyd's Newspaper " about the exe- cution. He said::—" It is not true. I know nothing whatever about it." He declined, however, to converse on the matter.

-Str Matthew White Ridley, the Home Secretary has, 'llowever relieved the public mind by the statement he made in the House of Commons on Tuesday with regard to the allr,ged bungling at the execution. The only foundation for the sensational stories which have ga abroad is, it appears, that the aseista,nt executioner did not step back as piornptly as he ought, and fell into the pit with the condemned men when the bolt was drawn. Though this is an exceedingly unfortunate incident, it is much less serious than what we were led to suppose -11 ad occurred.

THE MIN OF ME MOMENT.,

R. C. ARTHUR PEARSON, the well-known eiitor and puMisher,is just about 'o turn his business into a limited ibility c,mpany. Most of our remlers are no doubt interested in one or more of the many pepular periodicals published by Mr. Pearson, and will therefore take a perscnal and perhaps financial interest in the new company. The few notes published below of Mr. 'Pearsori's private life and public work which we gather from the •'Stationer and 'Fancy Trades Regis- ter," will probably, therefore, be read with special in- terest.

Mr. Cyril ArthurTearson was born on February 24, ,18e8, and is therefore now thirty. The place of his birth as Vi'ookey, near Wells, also famous for the manufacture of excellent hand-made paper. The son and gracdson of a country clergyman, it was intended that Mr. Pearson should continue the clerical traditions of his ancestors. But fate had willed otherwise, and it happened that shortly after he had left Winchester -College in 1884, a big competition was annwinced in "Tit Bits "—a journalistic venture of the theu George Ne vr nes. Mr. Newnes had offered a situation in his office, with .a salary of £lOO a year to the winner of a competition ,in his Inquiry Column. Mr. Pearson determined to try for this:price and for thirteen weeks be persevered in answering the questions asked. In the end he came out first of over 3,000 competitors, and so in due course entered Mr. Newnes' employ ai conductor-of the depart- , ment in connection With which he had so successfully studied. I For more than six years Mr. Pearson worked hard with Mr.-Newnes, becomiag the most useful member of his staff, and introducing many new ideas. A diversity of opinion came about when Mr. Pearson and Mr. Newnes differed as to the adf,quaey of the for- mer's salary, whereupon the subject of our sketch promptly said good-bye to A' Tit-Bits" and Mr. Newnes, and sought pastures new. Assisted by Mr. K.ary and one other colleague on " Tit-Bits," Mr. Pearson took two rooms in temple Chambers, where he commenced the publication of " Pearson's Weekly," now sufficiently well known by its P.W." With its red colour, twice the size of any contemporary, the A' Weekly " went up in circula- tion by leans and bounds. The palatial buildings in Henrietta-et., W.C. which Mr. Pearson has occupied since outgrowing the rooms in Temple Chambers, are now becoming all too small for the conduct of his many and increasing successful ventures. The subject of our sketch was known at sebool as an unusually bright boy,and carried off the form prizes at Winchester Collegeseveral times. _ _

All his life Mr.- Pearson had had an intense love of games, and it may be truly said that the successes of " Pearson's Weekly" and " Pearson's Magazine" were won on the playing fields of Winchester. When but fifteen years of age, Mr. Pearson played cricket in the Redford County Eleven, and had he been able to devote the necessary time to the game he would no doubt have ,developed into one of our foremost cricketers. _ _

Mr. Pearson resides at Godalming, in Surrey, with his wife and three children, and his sporting prowess is known.fra beyond that district. Every outdoer game knows Mr. Pearson as an enthusiast. A late member of the Yeomanry Cavalry, he is a fine horseman and an ex- pert at tent -pegging and other military sports,whilst his 4ove of driving a tandem or four-in-hand is only equalled by his love or cycling. There are few better whius,and when in:unted on one of his eighteen-pound bicycles he can get over the roads,kowever hilly,at a good seventeen or eighteen nv:les an hour. He is also an adept at lawn tencis and scientific croquet, plays a good game of golf, shoots straieht, and is, moreover, skilled at such indoor games as billiards and whist. In short, Mr... Pearson is one °Mose lucky men to whom games and snorts of all kinds 66M a second nature. Mr. Peter Keary'is Mr. Pes.rson's right band man, and has worked with him for more than twelve years. No one has a clearer insight as to what Class of matter the reading public most appreciates, and no one works harder to carry out his ideas. It is row proposed, under the title of " C. Arthur Pearson,Linsited," to purchase and carry on the business of newsroom and magazine printers, publishers, and proprietors, and to acquire the following periodicals : PEARSON'S WEEKLY, with its circulation closely verging upon half-a-million ; PEARSON'S MAGAZINE, than which but one other magazine commands a larger sale: HOME Nores. the pioneer of popular ladies' pipers, Hone COOKERY, SHORT STORIES, DRESSMAKING AT HOME, and FAsannes FOR CHILDREN. The Company will also acquire a large interest in the well-known musical magazine MeLonr, which is managed by Mr. Pearson. Mr. Pearson, it is reassuring to learn, will have the controlling interest in the business under his title of managing Director." He will have on the Board the ben fit tof the advice of his co-directors-6ir William Ingram. Bart. (chairman), the chairman and manager of the "Illustrated London News" Limited • his ex- perienced colleague, Mr. Peter Keary ; and Mr. A. W. Stirling. In reference to the aouudness of the concern, it is a matter of great significance to note that the bankers of the Company are to be the Bank of England, and that the auditors are to be the renowned firm of Messrs. Price. Waterhouse asei Co. A success as a private concern ought undoubtedly to be still more a success as a public enncern, and we have little doubt that this truism will lee. borne out by C. Arthur Pearson, Limited.

Y:M.C.A, GARDEN PARTY.

VISIT TO GRAPPENHALL HEYES.

Perhaps (tile of the most successful garden parties which has been held in connection' with the Warringtor4 branch of the Young Men's Christian Association wa, that which took place at Grappenhall Hayes on Satur- day afternoon. The members proceeded thence at the kind invitation of the President, Mr. J. Charlton Parr. J.P., DL., and Mrs. Parr. The event was favoured with fine weather, and no fewer than 230 persons res- ponded to the invitation. A start was made from the headquarters of the A sociation at 2 30, and ad the large pasty proceeded in conveyances through the streets, it attracted a great deal of attentien. The journey,beyond 'ef3B or two evidences of an inclination on the part of the horses to stop, was accomplished without any occui r- -1-once of note. On arrival at the hall, a reception was held, and the visitors were introduced to Mr. and Mrs. Parr by Mr. Osborne, the energetic secretary of the Acsoeiation. Proceeding through the entrance hall to the reception room one was struck with the wealth of pic- tures, curios, handsomely carved furniture, and adorn- ments to be seen on every hand. An inspection was afterwards made of the conservatories, and the party was then left to ramble through the extensive grounds and to indulge in various games uctil tea-time. A walk throngh the grounds was one of the most enjoyable features of the visit. They are admirably kept and reflect great credit upon the head gardener, Mr. Thompson. Shrubs, flowers, and plants were to be seen in a flourishing condition, and the air was scented with the perfume of roses of num- berl-ss tints. In the greenhouses, some fine specimens of orchids were conspicuous,and in the vineries,apricots, grapes and figs were being cultivated with as great a success as under sunnier skies. The aviary contained some specimens of that highly esteemed bird by epicures —the quail. A pleasant nook was the fernery, near to which a number of water-birds were tea be seen swim- ming on a pond, shaded by the overhanging branches of oak and other trees. Those of the party who were fond of angling were given permission to fish in the poolssand some good catches were made. Others engaged in lawn tennis, croquet, or bowls, whilst the youthful element played football in an adjoining field. Some others pre- ferred to sit down and listen to the music discoursed by the St. Paul's brass band, under the conductorship of Air. Smith. During the afternoon, Mr. and Mrs. Parr Mr. Roger Parr and Mr. Lister Kaye en- deavoured in every possible manner to make the garden t arty a success. At four o'clock, tea was provided in a tent erected in front of the house. The catering was in the hands of Mr. John Tort, of Bridge-street, and he gave every satisfaction. The tea tables were pre- sided at by Mrs. Parr, Mrs. John Fairclough, Miss Davies. Mrs. S. B. Stringer, Miss Shaw Green, and Miss Richards. The party afterwards assem- bled near the tennis court and was photographed by Mr. Cavanagh. Amongst those present in addition to Mr. and Mrs. Parr and Mr. Roger Parr, were Mr. and Mrs. John Fairclough, Mr. R. A. Naylor, Mr. William Owen, J.P., Dr. Starkey Smith, J.P., Rev. J. Davies v (vicar of St. Ann's),Miss Dies,Councillor Shaw Green,2 Mr. Wyllie (chairman of the Executive Committee of the Y.M.C.A), Mr. S. B. S ringer, Mr. J. Taylor, Mr.Bayley, Mr. Pickferd, Mr. Mounfield, Mr. John S. Green, Mr. Holford, and Mr. E. Osborne (general secretary of the Association). Whilst the party was assembled in a group,_elr._Parr was thanked for his hospitality.

_ Mr. R. WYLLIE said the present time was a favourable opportunity for unitedly expressing their thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Charlton Parr for the privilege and treat which they were all enjoying that afternoon. The winter months were the time of activity and general interest among the members, there being fewer attractions outside and cooler evenings to draw them together inside. They were looking forward to this next winter session with great expectations. Preparation was already being made. and they wanted every young man to feel that it was their Association,and cons, quently their interest and co- operation which were the ground work of success. (Ap- plause.)lt was more difficult to keep up the same amount of enthusiasm during the warm and relaxing summer months when every temptation was to get outside into the open air. They were then more apt to lose touch with each other. Still they had their cricket, lawn ten- nis, and cycles all on the move, with a crowded P.S.A. and Bible Class going on during the summer. It was a great pleasure for all of them to be given the opportu- nity of meeting with their President and his good lady on their (awn beautiful lawns. (Applause.) They must not suppose that bnause they did not often see their Presi- dent at Friars' Green that he did not visit and interest himself in the Association. Mr. Parr was more frequently there than some of them thought of. As the most powerful levers worked from below, even so their president's work and interest was more below than above the surface. Such an outing as that htd created a wonderful flutter of interest both inside and outside the Association, and they felt its effect in the numbers of young men who were enrolling as members each month. (Applause.) Their hope for the future was bright so far as the working success of the A,socia- don was concerned, Lnd they felt sure that if the work- ing success was attained, with Gld's blessing the finan- cial success would also be gained. (Anplause.) Let them all give their time, interest and talents to the furtherance of the yree, noble, and world-wide_ institu;

tion of which their Y.M.C.A. was a branch. He pro. posed that they accord to Mr. and Mrs. Charlton Parr a

hearty vote of thanks for their kindness in inviting them to such a beautiful garden party, and might they long be spared to bestow blessings and joys on those around them and receive the greater encomium from the Master, "Well done good and faithful servants." (Applause.) p Councillor &raw GREEN soconded the motion. He said he was disappointed that Mr. John Faircleugh, who had been with them that afternoon, and who was one of their vice-presidents, was not now present to fulfil that duty. It was but right that the Chairman of the Executive Committee should pro- pose a zee of thanks and it would also have been seemly iMeir Senior Vice-President. Mr. Fairclough, had seconded it. Hqwever, in Mr. Fairclough's absence he had much pleasure in discharging the duty. Them were Many present that evening who did not know of the beauties which surrounded that old town of theirs. They imagined that because it contained large works and had much smoke in it that they had none of the beauties of nature around them that were to be seen in places far distant. While standing on that lawn they could well imagine they were many miles from a town, although they were comparatively near to one. Ho was proud that Mr. Parr, in the goodness of his heart and with a strong desire to benefit the association ,had opened his charming gardens for their pleasure, thus giving them an opportunity for enjoying the beauties around them. Be (the spoaker) knew no institution which was calculated to benefit young people like the Y.M.C.A. They were banded together with a strong desire to pro- mote each o hers Christian welfare and material bene- fit. As a Corporatien they were doing very much to give the young people an opportnnity of receiving tech- nical and scientific instruction. They developed these advantages to the utmost of their power. He was , proud to he associated with the gathering that day, and felt that their thanks were greatly due to Mr. Parr, who had not only the ability but the spirit to give them that glorious treat. (Applause.) Mr. PICHFORD spoke on behalf of the P.S.A. in con- nection with the Association,and in a humorous manner supported the motion. The Rev. JAMES DAVIES said from the bottom ef his heart he wished the members " God speed " in the grand work in which they were engaged. ,He wished he could come amongst them a little more, but, as they knew, he had the respoosibility of a large and* poor parish, and it involved F Lich a great deal of work that he had little time for other duties. He was thinking that if he got a few more invitations to attend parties like the present he should regard them as permanent institutions, like Walking Day. (Laughter.) He most heartily sup- ported the vote of thank. (Applause.) Mr. W. 11. BAYLEY remarked that the frie.mbers of the association knew the practical interest which Mr. Parr took in the association ; he was sure they could not have a better president, and they loved him and appreciated all he had done for them. He (the 'speaker) was one of their Oelegates to the conference at Derby last week, where 500 representatives of Y.M.C. Ass )ciations were assembled from all pasts of the world. He did not think the members of the association in Warringten under- stood the vast importance of the work which these associations were tryieg to carry out. They only did work in a very small and humble way in Warrington, whereas in many large cities and towns in England it was of vast importance. (Hear' hear.) On behalf of the !young men of the Association he had pleasure in sup- porting the resolution.

Mr. PARR, on rising to respond, was greeted with cheer*: • He-said it gave Mrs. Parr and himself the greatest pleasure to see them gathered that day, and to hear that they had all enjeyed themselves. Be was not a man of many words,and what he did not say he hoped they would take for granted. He felt very grateful to them for the kind manner in which his name had been received and big poor services recognised. He confessed that he did talc° a very great interest in the association, and hoped he should continue to do so, and that he would see it in even a renre prosperous condition than it was that day. But the poor services he was able to render were nothing compared with those rendered by the Executive Cornin:ttee, including such men as Mr. Wyllie and Mr. Oshorrie.9.ho gave their time and talents and worked,its someone had said,from below. They did not show half the work they did on the sut face. It was thePe persons who really kept the association entre and to whom their best thanks were due. They must all remember that it was the members who made the Y. M.C.A. and not the Y.M.C.A. who made tile members. They ought to do their best to bring up the association to the highest pinnacle of efficiency and respectability, and every other virtue titey could suggest:* As they all knew, these agsociations were worked admirably in Lon- don. They were very powerful institutions, and the head of them had been recognised by the Qeeen for the valuable services he had rendered to the country. (Applause.) Let them all emulate the example which had been set by the London institutions, and make up their minds that Warrineton should not be far behind them. Let every mernher make up his mind that it de- pended upon his exertions as to how soon perfecVon was reached—if it ever could be reached in this world. He

thanked the ladies for having attended that day and for having by their presence—as one of the speakers had said—lent SO much beauty to the proceedings. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) He had partly in deference to the ladies asked to he allowed to introduce an innovation which, lie hop d, would not be frowned upon by the as- eciation, and that was, that as soon as the meeting ternamated that the hind should strike up scene lively runes,and their friends should engage in a dance. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. OSBORNE' said ee was glad to have that opportun- ity of thanking personally Mr. and Mrs. Parr for the manner they had entertained the members of the Association that day. He stated that Mr. Parr had been the president of the aseociation since its formation, and Mrs. Parr had shown her deep interest in it by acting on 'he ladies.auxlary. Mr. Lister Kaye had willingly helped them that day in their prep,irations, and as he and the ladies who had presided at the tea tab:es had worked har I, the best thanks were due to them. These associa- tions were growing rapiellv. The latest return showed that the work of the Y.NI.C.A. was represented through- out the world by 6,232 centres, being an increase of 497 centres for their la-t year. The distribution was as fol- lbws :—Etsrope 4,545, American, 1,456 Africa, 19, Asia, 188, Oceana 24. 'I he gross membership now exceeded the half million, the exact fi4ures representing the totals for the different countries being 511.750 an increase of membership for the past year 05 19,165. The work was carried on by or on behalf of the young manhor d by- over 40 different nationalities, and it was a pronounced success. They in Warrington were rapidly growing,the membership was increasing,and 'Any intended to branch out and work more extensively and exhaustively for time Master. It was certainly a good work in which they were engaged. It had been saki that anyone who joined such an aseociation ss as namby-pamby. (Laughter.) But that was a ridiculous idea. He should like to know if their founder, who was One of the most suecessful business men isr all London, was namby-pamby? (Ap- plause.) They aimed at being good young men, not goody-goody men, and they wished to show that while they were Christians they could enjoy themselves as they had been doing that Afternoon. (Applause.) The band afterwards played fer dancing which was con- tinued on the tennis ground until about 7.30, when the party again entered tha conveyances, and a start was made for home. As the party moved off, cheers and counter cheers were given for Mr. and Mrs. Parr.

OUR LOCAL CIIURCHES-WARRINGTON-

SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY AT LATCHFORD.

On Sunday, the anniversary services in connection with the vVesleyan Church, Latchford were held. In the morning the services were conducted by the Rev. C. Andrew, of the Baptist Chapel, Latchford and he preached an excellent sermon. In the afternoon a 11 iwer service was held for parents and children, most of the children bringing large bun;hei of beautiful flowers. Mr. W. Hitehin delivered an address on "Flowers." The Misses flenshall, Hart, wad Whittaker had taken a great deal of trouble to tastefully decorate the chapel for the occasion, and it was consequently very bright and attractive. Mrs. Broadbent, of the- Hollies, had also kindly lent some plants. The service in the evening was conducted by Miss Dutton, of Moore, whose discourse proved of a vory attractive character; The sibging of the choir reflected great credit upon, their choirmaster, Mr. W. Ford,And it was evident that he had taken great pains to train them. The collections, which were in aid of the Sunday schools, amounted to £2l. There was a large attendance at all the services, but more esp2cially in the evening.

BRUNSWICK WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL. As will be geen by a reference to our advertising columns the 96th anniversary of Brunswick Wesleyan Sunday School, will take place on Sunday, when two sermons will be preached by the Rev. B. H. May. of Handsworth College, in St. Paul's Chapel, Runcorn. The Brunswick Schools bear the reputation of being 'the old- est and largest school in the locality and during the many years religious instruction has been going on in them much good must have been done. The Rev. H. H. May, who is a rising, young preacher of Methodism, will also preach on Monday night from the unusual subject "Red, White, Bay, Grisled and nick Horses." NEW TEACHERS AT RUNCORN.

_ A special meeting of the Runcorn School Band took' place last Saturday, the business being the filling of the vacancies on the Board teaching staff in Runcorn. Infants' School, salary £4O per annum. RUNCORN INDUSTRIAL BUILDING SOCIETY. Mr. E. Ridge presided over the annual meeting of this society which was held on Thursday. The eleventh annual report and balance sheet were submitted by Mr. J. E. Meredith, secretary. From the financial statement it was seen that the year commenced with a balance of £lBl 123. 4d., and £321 is. 91. had been received in members subscriptions, repayment of appropriation, Sze. On the adverse side £235 had been advanced on mortgage, £3O 93. Id. paid for working expenses and £228 14s. in withdrawals, leaving a balance in hand of £l4 103. In the revenue account the balances at the end of the year were shown at £1,328 93. 11d, due to shareholders and £2 193. ld. undivided profits, not including prospective profit which was estimated at £4lO. Messrs. John A. Goodwell and Richard KtileTOSS were re-elected directors and Messrs. W. Handley and Joseph Pritchard were gain appointed auditors. FLOWER SERVICES AT BETHESDA CHURCH, RUNCORN. In connection with tbe Bethesda Congregation Church, Runcorn, flower services were held last Sunday. The services both morning and evening were well attended, the congregation in the evening being an exceptional gathering. The decorations were well up to the stand- ard of previous years, the interior of the church being tastefully decorated. The pulpit was neatly garbed with lilies, creepers and oranee blossoms, while the table fronting the pulpit bore many beautiful bouquets, lavis- hly bestowed by the members of the congregation. The whole of the decorations were creditable to the skilful hands of those who assisted,to whom every praise is due. The set mons were preached by the pastor,. the Rev. J. Simpson, who in the evening preached, under the title of "A Summer Sermon," an appropriate address. Special music was rendered by the choir and solos were given by Mrs. Timperley. The flowers,after the iservices, were distributed amongst the sick, while a portion was I reserved for the Widnes Hospital. ST. EDWARD'S CATHOLIC CHURCH, RUNCORN. The anniversary serums in connection with the Wes- leyan Schools were preached on Sunday in the above church. The preacher—the Rev. Father Day, in the morning,took for his subject, "The scandal of our little ones," and dealt at censiderable length with the late Education Bill, and the attitude of the Board schools to religious teaching. The preacher pointed out the cruel disparity between board and voluntary sehools, and said the former did not supplement, but on the contrary rather superseded the latter, which was an infringement of the rights of the Church. The schools were a scandal because they had no thfiriite religious system, and this was the actual state of education given to.over 2,000,000 children in Christian England. Toe musical part of the service consisted of "Kyrie" and "Gloria" from Gosinod's "Messe Solennelle," and selections from Turner's "Missa de Santa Cecilia " ; with the offertory "0 Jesu bone pastor." Glover. The solos were rendered by Miss A. Richardson (Warrington). Messrs. Dockrey, and Lycns. In the evening the Rev. Father Day took for his subject "Of such is the Kingdom of Heaven."

THE EXAMINER SATUR IC, JULY 4.

RUNCORN LIRERAL ASSOCIATION.

On Wednesday evening a meeting under the auspices of the above Association was held in the Liberal club. The business was private. We understand the chief items were to consider whether to fight all future elec- tions on political lines,and also to suggest that a fund be raised at once to place the party finances on a satisfac- tory basis.

BOWLING MATCH AT RUNCORN

On Thursday afternoon Messrs. Thomas Dean and William Sherratt,two well-known Runcorn bowlers met on the Wilson's Hotel Bowling Green to decide a private match. There was a good attendance and an interest- ing game was witnessed. The terms of the match were 81 up fur £5 aside, and Mr. Dean ran up to his total while his opponent compiled 73. The game resulted in favour of the first-named by 7 points. GIRLS' FRIENDLY SOCIETY PICNIC, RUNCORN.

—.— On Wednesday afternoon about 120 members of the Girls' Friencily S)ciety in connection with the Parish and St. Michael's Churches, Runcorn, held their annual pic- nic. The party travelled to Overton in conveyances supplied by Messrs. Booth and Sons. At Overton service was held in the church. After which the rest of the afternoon was spent in the pleasure resorts of the neigh- bourhood. The Rev. W. R. Staples was in charge of the party, which returned to Runcorn shortly after eight o'clock.

The quarterly meeting of the Runcorn Wesleyan Cir- cuit was held on Monday-afternoon. Aboir 50 members were present to whom tea was supplied by Mesdames Rawlinson, F. J. Norman, George and Glicidon. Subse- quently the meeting was held over which the Rev. T. Brackenbury presided. The stewards (Messrs. Rawlin- son and Norman, presented their report which was read and adopted. RUNCORN COUNTY COURT.

TUESDAY. —Before his Honour Judge. Wynne-Ffoulkes.

A DISPUTE OVER GROCERIES.—WiIIiaIII Shore, grocer and chaese dealer, Frodshan2 Bridge, sued William Fearnett, for a sum of £3 103. • 6d., value of groceries supplied. Plaintiff, his wife and daughter severally proved the delivery ol the goods and the non-payment of the accounts sent in, and defendant denied contract- ing the debt or the receipt of the groceries. In support of his statement he called his wife, who also denied giv- ing the order or receiving the goods. His Honour gave judgment for the pla ntiff for the amount claimed, to be naid at the rate of 53. per month. A FEATHER BED STORY.—Edward Clark. of Kingswood farm, Frodsham, claimed £1 163. from Jane Bradburn, Peel-st., Runcorn, for feathers supplied. Defendant did not deny the purchasing of the feathers, but said she took them on the understanding that they were dressed. When the feathers were delivered they turned out to be undressed, and she had to get assistance to do the dres sing 'nerself. Evidence was called in support of he statement, but his Honour gave judgment for the amount claimed, pointing out that defendant ought to have examined the feathers before she concluded the bargain.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. McDougal reported that there had been 24 deaths registered during the last six months. One was an in- quest case,and of the 23 remaining 4 were duet° cancer, 8 to pneumonia, 3 to infantile diarrhoea, 2 to infantile convulsions, 2 to congenital debility, 1 each to typhoid fever, phithis, tubercular meningitis, senile decay, heart disease, pleurisy,sunstroke,and nervous disease. The 24 deaths show an average death rate for the 12 months of 15.5, and exclusive of the inquest case 149. Sixty-two births were registered during the month.

HIGHWAYS AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE.

This Committee recommended the adoption of the fol- lowing plans :—Of Mr. William Rowland 4of two stables with harness rooms and coach houses proposed to be erected at the rear of houses in Moughland-lane ; of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and C0.,0f internal alterations at the Nelson Inn, High-st. ; of Mr. D. Watkin of a'aingle-storeyed shop proposed to be erected in Church- street ;of Messrs. W. Farrell and Son of 17 houses pro- posed to be erected in the Holloway. (Original plan passed on the 14th May) ; of Mr. J. J. Bowyer of two cottages proposed to be erected at the junction at Heath- road and Victoria-road.

The recommendations were confirmed on the motion of Mr. Bisbrown seconded by Mr. Speakman.

HEALTH COMMITTEE.

This committee recommended that Mr. James Perci- val be appointed inspector of nuisances for ths urban district of Runcorn at a salary at the rate of £125 a year, subject to the appointment being determined on one month's notice being given. Mr. Handley moved the adoption of the minutes of the Health Committee and pointed out that Mr. Percivll would by the foregoing resolution be a permanent !Ali- cia' on the same tooting as the Council's other officials. With reference to the medical officer's report be might supplement it by saying that the notification of infec- tious diseases had been very satisfactory. Three months ago 23 cases of infectious diseases were notified, last month the members dropped to nine and this month it had been reduced still further to four. Four of the wards had been entirely without infectious disease. There had been one case of typhoid fever in the Halton Ward, and three cases of scarlet fever in the Mersey Ward.—Mr. Jones seconded the minutes, which were passed*

WATER COMMITTEE. The Water Committee recommended that the tender of the Staveley Coal and Iron Co. for the supply of 309 yards 3" iron pipes and 100 yards of 6" iron pipes be accepted, and that a 6" sluice valve be obtained from the Glenfield Company.—Confirmed on the motion of Mr. Pritchard. MARKETS AND CEMETERY COMMITTEE. The Markets and Cemetery Committee recommended that the following be the scale of charges for the use of the fire engine and apparatus in respect of fires lccurr- ing outside the urban district : Three guineas when the engine and apparatus is conveyed to a fire but not used. Four guineas when used for any perio4 not exceeding three hours ' • Five guineas when used for any period exceeding three hours. Dr. Grastaldi moved the adoption of tho minutes and Mr. Jones seconded. Mr. Dean asked a ques floc respecting the scale of chsrges for the use of the fire engine in the country dis- tricts. He wished to know whether the scale had been altered or not. For his own part he did not see why they should be so very modest in their charges to country dis- tricts. He took it that they did not contribute to their rates and lie understood the Council had been in the habit of charging £5 fist* the use of the engine.—Mr. Bisbrown said that when the matter cane under con- sideration it was felt that £5 was an exorbitant charge. Complaints had been laid to the Council by people who had used the engine, for a short time, and they found they were in excess of other districts in charging. The present scale was laid on the basis of scales, used by other authorities.—A member asked whether that included the horse hire.—Mr. Bisbrown in reply said that it was exclusive of horse hire for which they had a special resolution.—Mr. Dean said had he known that he I would not have asked the question, as he was basing his I calculations on the horse hire.

SPECIAL BUSINESS.

The Chairman reported that he had received the fol- lowing letter from the Local Government Board :—" I am directed by the Local Government Board to state that at a joint request of the Town Councils of Man- chester and Salford and the Urban District Council of Stretford. the Board had withdrawn and revoked the order which they had made on the 23rd March, 1896, and which became provisional for the construction of a permanent Port Sanitary Authority for the port of Manchester, and I am to forward for the informa- tion of the Urban District Council of Runcorn, the enclosed draft, of an order which the Board propose to mak. setting up as a temporary port sani- tary authority for a period of three years. a joint board, consisting of representatives of the Town Councils of Manchester and Salford, and the urban district of Stret- ford.—Mr.Speakman considered that the letter was very satisfactory to the committee which had had the matter in hand.—The Chairman was empowered to affix the seal of the Council to a contract with James Allen, senior and Sons.—Dr. Gastaldi asked whether the com- mittee formed a few months ago to enquire into the trade of the town had done anything.—fhe Chairman said they had m..t several times and had a number of suggestions before them.—The meeting then came to a conclusion. .Zioraalia

NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL,

BUSINESS OF THE NEXT MEETING.

A meeting of this Council will be held in Ear:estown Town Hall on Monday next when the following among other business will be sebmitted. SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE.-MORE CORRESPONDENCE. This Committoe reports the receipt of another letter from the Education Department stating that if the managers of a school fail to give such information as is required to enable the School Attendance Committee to ascertain whether a child, resident in their district and attending that school, attends the same, they must cause to be produced to a duly authorised officer or member of the Committee at any reasonable time, the registers, &c., containing information with respect to the attendance of children and shall permit him to in- spect and take copies of and extracts from the same. A letter had also been received from the Rev. J. J. Lennon (managars' correspondent of St. Mary's and St. John's Roman Catholic day schools, Newton-le-Willows) stating that he had received a letter from the Education depart- ment; he also etated that the managers of the school had fixed the first and third or second and fourth Wed- nesdays of each month at 4.15 p.m.as the time when the school attendance officer of the committee could attend at their schools and ascertain the names of absentees,but that the managers would arrange that a pupil or assist-' ant teacher should be present to see that no further use was made of the registers. He also enclosed a copy of a letter which be stated would be forwarded by that even- ing's post to the Education Department. After consider- ing the foregoing letters the committee resolved that the Clerk be directed to acknowledge the receipt of the letter dated June 12th, from the Education Department, and to thank them for so promptly advising the commit- tee upon the:matter at issue, and that he enclose copy of the letter received from the managers' correspondent of Roman Catholic Day Schools, as well as a copy of the one which he stated was about to be sent up to the Education Department, both of which bore date of June 15th, 1896. It was also resolved that the second Wed- nesday in each month, at 4.15 p.m.,be the day and time when the school attendance officer shall present himself at the Roman Catholic day schools for the purpose of being supplied with, or of obtaining such information as the committee are legally entitled to, under section 22, Elementary Act, 1873,as set forth in the letter from the Education Department of June 12th, 1896. HIGHWAYS, PUBLIC WORKS AND SANITARY COMMITTEE. This committee report the receipt of a letter dated June Bth, from Mr. Pardey, Lord Newton's agent, in answer to the Clerk's letter of the 4th of June informing him that a numerously signed petition had been addressed to the Chairman of the Council calling atten- tion to the insanitary and dangerous state of the pit at the junction of Crow-lane and Market-street. Mr. Pardey stated that he would give instructions for the pit to be fenced off or filled up. A letter was read from Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons calling attention to the disgraceful state of the footpath in Faircicugh-street. After a discussion,it was proposed by Mr. Borron, seconded by Mr. Thompson "that the footpath on one side of Fairclough-street be paved." An amendment was proposed by Mr. Gilmour, seconded By Mr. Ormand, "That the matter be deferred until the private streets within the district requiring attention are completed." Five votes were recorded for the amendment and four for the motion. The Chairman declared the amend- ment to be carried. A letter was read from Captain Royse, R.N. Newton- le-Willows, complaining that on the 18th of jay one of the Council's officials had allowed men and animals con- nected with the Fair to make use of the private road past his house. The Clerk was directed to say that the Committee had given instructions to prevent a recur- rence of his complaint. Tenders for carting as may be required by the Com- mittee for one year commencing July let, 1898, were submitted. A letter dated May 30th, 1896, was read from Mr. R. Jones of Bridge-St.. Earlestown (the present contractor for carting), reminding the Committee that his contract had yet two years to run. The Committee examined the contract signed by Mr. Jones. After a eiscussion it was resolved—that the tender of Mr. R. Jones be recommended to the Council for acceptance, provided he would agree to the contract being for a period of three years, commencing July Ist, 1896, at the prices quoted in his tender. Mr. Gregory, the deputy sanitary inspector, in his report states that the district is now quite free from cases of infectious diseases, though there are a few cases of scarlet fever in hospital. His monthly tolls for street hawking are £3 7s. 11d.;market tolls, £26 103. 3d.•' total £29 183. 2d. That amount includes £7 53., tolls for Newton horse and cattle fair. For the corresponding per- ind of last year the tolls were £29 53. 7d.,which included £7 10s. 9d. for tolls at the fair. Several nuisances were reported, and it was resolved that seven days' notice for their abatement should be served. Building plans were considered with reference to which it was resolved—that plans for a new Post Office at Newton-le-Willows, and of alterations and additions to the Railway Hotel, Earlestown, be approved and passed. Tenders for the erection of closets on the market were submitted, and it was resolved that the tender of Elis Bunting for the erection of closets on the Market for the sum of £B6 10s. be accepted. It was resolved that Messrs. Gilmour. Simpson, and MacMahon, with the chairman of ,the committee, be a sub-committee re stalls in the market.

SHIP CANAL. TRADE FOR THE HALF YEAR.

Dwring the month of June 734 seagoing vessels, with an aggregate net registered tonnage of 189,159 tons, ar- rived at and sailed from the port of Manchester,bringing up the total seagoing traffic for the half year ended on Tuesday last to considerably over a million net regis- tered tons. The record of the port for the last six months is one of steady, and, on the whole, rapid pro- gress, although in one or two directions its development has been retarded by unfriendly influence. Thus the effort made by the Levant meichants to obtain a dirt ct steamship service from the canal failed in consequence of a small but influential minority supporting the Conference shipowners in their hostile attitude. aimilarly the China trade, which a year ago was of great importance and promise, has been almost entirely rediverted t,) Birkenhead by the shipowners, acting in concert. This loss will, it is believed, be only tempor- ary, as arrangements are being made for a powerful Japanese steamship company, unfettered by Conference restrictions, to establish an ooposition service between Manchester and the Far East. In nearly every other direction unmistakable success has attained the efforts of the Canal management to attract traffic. In the other long-distanced trades, almost without exception, the volume of traffic has increased in a gratifying manner.

THE NORTHERN RUGBY FOOTBALL UNION.

At a meeting of the Northern Rugby Football Union held at Huddersfield on Thursday night, the following new ckubs were admitted :—Swinton Church, Runcorn Recreation, and Runcorn Victoria. The committee ruled that the presentations made from the club funds to re- tired players constituted an act of professionalism on the part of both players and club. A. letter was read from the Wigan shop assistants, asking the union to form a Wednesday League. The committee decided that, though they could not form the proposed league, they would give the movement all the encouragement , they could. The rules of the Union,by laws of counties, and of challenge cup competitions proposed to be insti- tuted were revised.

SUNDAY SCHOOL TREAT AT DARESBURY.

On Saturday, about 170 of the Church Sunday School scholars attending the schools at Daresbury, Moore, Preston Brook and Hatton, were given an excellent tea by Mrs. William, C. Jones, of the Oak- lands. At three o'clock the scholars with their teachers arrived at Daresbury School, and com- menced amusing themselves in a field kindly placed at their di,posal by Mr. Joseph Billington. A little later the Warrington Blue Coat School band arrived, adding to the children's enjoyment, and after tea, cricket and games of all kinds were indulged in, including racing, tugs-of-war, &c., for which money prizes were given.
