On Monday morning. Joeph Stafford, 5, Cambria- place, expired suddenly at his residence. He was taken ill on Friday night but it was thought not seriously and no doctor was sent for until Monday morning when it was no'iced by those who Were watching him that his condition was precarious. Despite all their efforts, however, he expired about a quarter-past nine. lie was for many years em- ployed by Messrs. Pilkington as a carter. An in- quest on the body was held on 'I tiesday at the Borough Police Court, before Mr. Brighouse. The coroner said in thi- case there. had been rumours that deceased had stated that he received his in-

juries in a fight with another man. Inquiries, had been made into the matter. Dr. Ernest Bowden, deposed to reeking a post-mortem examination on the deceased and found internally that there were signs of pleurisy of some duration. Both lungs showed chronic bronchitis. The heart was dilated. Dr. Campbell, wbo bad been attending the deceased for some years, said he had been suffering from a dilated heart and chronic bronchitis. Mary Pierce, a stepdaughter of the deceased, gave evidence as to finding her father dead in bed. The jury found that the deceasei died from "Natural causes." War-

LAWN MO WE RS.—GARLAND, Bridg e-street, rington ,—Advt. BOATING ACCIDENT AT PADDINGTON LOCKS

_ On Sunday evening at Paddington Locks, a skiff was hired from Mr. Platt in which three men, a , boy about twelve years of age and a baby twelve months old started for a row on the river. About 5.15 p.m. they were in that part of the river near Paddington Locks. when it is stated one of the party got up to step out on to an old bat lying near the side. In doing so the boat capsized and the occupants were all thrown into the water. A. man on the bank jumped in.with the object of rescu- ing those in the water, but, unfortunately, he was soon in difficulties himself, and calling lot help. An oil boat from Manchester, the 14 umber, was just being locked in the Bridgewater Canal at the time, and the Lockma-ster (Mr. John Rathbone), his son (Mr. Peter Rathbone), Mr. Thomas Ward (the captain), the engine driver (Thomas Bent), and a man named Cornelius Kinsey gave timely assist- ance. Those in the water were in a critical posi- tion, and Mr. Rathbone lost no time in reaching the baby, who was resting on the chest of one of the men, with a boat hook. The others were in turn taken from the water, one man appearing to be suffering from the effects of his immersion,alt hcu,:h the other men did not appear any the worse. Mr. Peter Rathbone, who was a member of the Pad- gate C.L.B. Ambulance Class, rendered what assist- ance was necessary to those taken from the water. "AN ARTFUL DoDGER"

On Friday night a man dressed in seafaring cos- tume was giving exhibitions of feats of strength on the Earlestown Market-place before admiring crowds. After finishing his performance there he got into Market-street, and allowed himself to be tied to a lamp-post, undertaking to free himself. He was however, unable to do so, and his furtile efforts caused considerable amusement. Sergeant Banks eventually released the man from his bonds. The strong man ultimately set out for Warrington. but on arriving in Wargrave-road he evidently be- came disheartened with the task of having to trudge a weary five miles at nearly midnight. Con- seluently he either had a fir, or cleverly simulated one, with the result that the whole neighbourhood was aroused, including P.C. Schofield. All efforts to restore the man were however without avail,and Mr. T. Bowes, relieving officer, was sent for. The latter procured a cab,and the man having been lifted in, Mr. Bowes drove off with him to the War- rington workhouse. When just entering Warring- ton the man suddenly revived, and Mr. Bowes is new inclined to think that the man was an old dodger and knew how to obtain a free ride to his destination.

Carrington was solely responsible for advice to .the Government in regard to the forces required to put down the rebellion in Rhodesia. Bare the rebellion began the Imperial Government had taken ever the so 4 responsibility for the maintenance of peace and order Tn Rhodesia. The Lord A.dvacate (Mr. A. G. MURRAY), in asking for leave to introduce a bill for Scotland similar to the Agri- cultural Land Rating Bill for England, said the object of the English bill was to redress the inequality existing between personalty and reality in the matter of local taxation, and the application of relief to the agricul- tural class which most suffered. In applying that relief to•S3otland they could not follow the lines ok the Eng- lish bill, because the Scotch system of rating was so differeht, and they were therefore driven logically in the Scotch case to the principal of an equivalent grant. They proposed to give £214,500. Tne bill was brought in and read a first time. _ _ _ .

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

lINHAPTY DOMESTIC RELATIONS AT WARRINGTON. Before the magistrates at the Warrington Borough Court on Thursday morning a man named Mallet, work- ing on the Ship Canal came and informed the bench that he was very much hurt about his wife'sconduct. He had been sent to prison for striking her and was determined to have done with it. He was afraid if things went on much longer he would lose his temper and strike her again. She had made a charge rgainst him which was entirely untrue and had threatened to strike him with a . bottle. Fiis children were also neglected. He wanted to know which course to pursue—The Rey. G. D. Mason told the magistrates that the man had teen much troubled by reports which his wife was circulating and as they were not true he had wished him (Mr_ Mason) to come to the court and help him. He thought that if he was to see the mants wife he might be able to do some- thing to meet the ca... If it was their wish he would do what he could.—The Chief Constable: You see hp makes no charge against his wife that we can notice.— " Applicant : I give her a lot of money and I don't know what she does with it. I have afrmany pawntickets in our house as would paper this place (" pointing round the court.)"—The magistrates said they thought the best thing would be for Mr. Mason to see the man s wife and try and smooth matters.

Tim AITEMPTED SUICIDE BY RAT POISON. At the Warrington Borough Court, on Tuesday, John Bolan, 56 years of age, of Back Dallam-lane, was brought up on remand an a charge of attempting to commit suicide on the 22nd June.—The Chief Conde?)le explained to the magistrates that the prisoner was re- manded in custody for a week in order that his condition might be attended to. He had got in a low nervous state, and had attempted to take his life by taking some rat poison.. The Chief Constable proceeded to recount the facts of the case as given in .our last issue, and stated that the prisoner seemed better that morning both mentally and phesically.—Mra. Bolan, wife of the prisoner, said that drink was the cause of her husband's conduct.—He now exelained his sorrow for what h* bad done, and promised not to repeat his foolish act- tion.—The magistrates discharged him. LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE COUNTY ASSOCIATION. ' THE AMATEUR CHAMPIONSHIP.

The seventh annual individual scratch contest, open to all affiliated amateurs in Lancashire and Cheshire, was commenced at Heaton Chapel on Saturday, and continued during the present week. Thy total of the entrants reached 128, a number in excess of previous years. The play of the past champions, all of whom contested, was watched with much interest, and three out of the five wire dismissed on taturday by heavy mijorities. The two Fieldings, of Lower Broughton, alone secured places in, the second round. Of those who gained awards in last season's individual merit competi- tion, the senior vice-president, who ran up to the final, came out of the ordeal with nine shots to spare ; R. W Munson. of Withington' distanced his opponent by seven ; A. Jones, of North Warrington,regained his posi- tion by eleven ; and R. Anderton enjoyed a walk over. Among the colts who made good headway were J. B. Shaw, of the Caledonians, who succeeded in putting in the heaviest majority in the day's performances. Re- sults :

THE PREVENTION OF CRUELTY TO

CHILDREN.

MEETING OF THE WARRING roN BRANCH. The annual meeting of the Warrington and district branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children was held in the Mayor's Parlour at the Town RaII, Warrington, on Friday. Captain Rodger occupied the chair, and there were present—Mes3rB. C. Harold Broadbent, W. H. Greener, and A. Fl. Lee, Mrs. James Fairclough (Mayoress), Mrs. E. Greenall, and Mrs. Harrison. WORE IN WARRINGTON DISTRICT. The fourth annual report stated that the committee had great pleasure in vending the beneficial work' accomplished in this district during the past year, and took that opportunity of congratulating the Society's members upon the success-of their efforts. It was a cause for regret that the number of cases which bad come before the Society's notice had increased considerably. I upon the previous year's returns. On the other hand,it I was a proof of the Society's activity in bringing to light what would otherwise remain obscure. Action had been taken in 65$ eases, involving 994 offenders, and chang- ing for the better 600 homes in this district since the branch was started four year ago. It had reached 2,046 children,and,in the name of the 1aw,445 warnings bad been given, 131 prosecutions bad been instituted, and 1,400 supervisiou visit's had been m -des Facts and figures however, left little impression upon the ordinary mit:El ; but these facts and fieures repre. sented pain, sorrow,and suffering inflicted upon helpless childhood at the hands of brutal, callous,and indifferent parents. The society's first object was to prevent , cruelty to cl-ildren. It would be noticed that the num- ber of prosecuted cases had greatly decreased. Only when warning proved ineffectual, which was very seldom, did the society avail itself of the strong arm of the law. Supervision followed a term of imprisonment, and helped parents to fulfil their good resolutiocs. When a case came under the notice of your committee, it was peri-dically supervised by the Inspector, and no case was finally struck off the books until it was deemed per- fectly setistactery. Financially, the year had been one of success, and the committee tools that opportunity of congratulating the many labourers on the fruits o their toil. To the subscribers and donors their sincerest thanks were again due, and they trusted that their past favours might be extended to the future. They had been able to hand over a balance to the National Society of £llO 16s. 4d.. to help to further develop the w rk in other parts of the country, to educate public opinion, and carry Bills through the Houses of Parlia- ment of ;lie greatest importanre in coon. ction with the further protection of the Nation's children. The committee tended its best thanks to Miss Broadbent for the energetic and able way in which she was carrying had on the Children's League of Pity, and to all those who helped in the great work they had in hand,and they wished in particular to thank the police generally, the Scheel Attendance officers. 4Sce , for their kind and con- tinued help. They also desired to express their apprecia- tion of the very satisfactory way in which Inspector McAteer had fulfilled his difficult and sometimes painful duties.

The report was adopted on the motion of Mr. C. H Broadbent, seconded by the Mayoress. The CHAIRMAN proposed a vote or thanks to the hon. treasurer and secretary, and referred in felicitous terms to their excellent work on behalf of the society.—Mr. A. H. Lee seconded, and said they were exceedingly fortunate in Warrington in having those who would see to the interests of the society.—Mr. Greener responded to the vote of thitnks, and the meeting concluded with a similar compliment being accorded the Chairman.

7Letterz to tor ebttor.

SANKEY BROOK. Sir,—l am glad someone has drawn attention through . yew columns to the wretched state of Sankey Brook. The stench arising from it is abominable.so much so that even during the hot weather which we recently experi- enced, we had to keep the windows and doors always closed as the -smell from the brook was worse than the inconvenience from the heat. And seemingly not con- tent with the existing nuisance, we are calmly told that the Rural Authority had under consideration the length of piping required for the conveyance of sewage into the brook. Surely some regard ought to be paid to the health of the inhabitants. It is well known that the • nuisance exists and yet no steps are taken to put it right. wonder then the locality has a bad name, and houses are empty which otherwise would be occupied. I hope that the matter will not be allowed to rest until the evil has been remedied.—Yeurs truly,

A SUFFERER:

ABOLITION OF WALKING DAY.

Sir,—Has not the time arrived for the abolition of Walking Day as a special festival for Warrington? I ask the question now in order to give ample time for the discussion of the subject before another year, and I trust the discussion will result in the discontinuance of the festival at this season of the year. It is passing strange that Warringten should give itself up to fes- tivity at a time when its neighbours are absorbed in business. What a dislocation of commercial arrange- ments it causes,and that for no earthly reason. If we are to keep holiday, let us do so when other people keep theirs, and we shall thus save much inconvenience. I have spoken to many persons lately on this subject, and they are agreed that Walking Day ought to be held on Whit-Monday. As it is,we are always having holidays. Nearly every month during the summer there is a holi- day—Easter, Whitsuntide, Walking Day, and August Bank Holiday—to the great injury of business. We have no sooner had one holiday than we are preparing for the next,and the consequence is that all through the summer business is at a very low ebb. Times are hard enough without having to suffer the stagnation caused by unnecessary holidays, and I trust the authorities will take steps to abolish a festival which has nlw no reason for its continuance as a special heliday.—Yours, ke.,

A TRADESMAN.

July 2nd, 1896. PARISH COUNCIL MEETINGS. GRAPPENHALL.

On Monday P vening the quarterly meeting of the Grappenhall Parish Council was held in the National Schools. There were present :—Messrs. J. Charlton Parr, D.L . Lister Kaye, Thomas Smith, R. L. Warbur- ton, R. Boardmin, John Bradford. F. Ellison. and S. J. Woodcoci: (clerk). Mr. Boardman was voted to the chair, and he thanked the members for the honour they had conferred upon him as their chairman.—Mr. William Long, J.P., accepted the position of vice-chairman.— Mr. Lister K ,ye complained of the condition of the Ship Canal, and suggested that the County Council should be approazhed and asked to take steps to cause the bad smell from the water to be done away with.— The Chairman said he had been moving in the matter for the past six montns. He had written to the Public Health Committee of the County Council and the River Pollution Committee, and he had also written to the Warrington Corporation, through the Mayor. The more they agitated in the matter the more they were likely to be attended to. Many of them knew that a great deal of sickness was about, caused by the bad smells. The Clerk said there were two cases of illness in the village which the doctor attributed to bad smells.—Mr. Smith observed that the Ship Canal Company were as anxious as anyone that the bad smells should be done away with —The Clerk —The Salford Corporation have been sum- moned for contributing to the nusisance, and they have promised to abate it so far as they are concerned. It is caused by the sewage floating into the river Irwell, and subsequently finding its way into the canal. Steps are being taken to do away with the nuisance by forming reservoirs and filtering. It is said that in eime the nuis- ance will be entirely done away with.—The Chairman. —Perhaps not for a generation. (Laughter.)—Mr. Smith —There is a divided area. The Canal is part in one county and part in another.:—After some further discus- sion the following resolution was passed "That a memorial he forwarded to the Public Health Committee and the Rtvers Pollution Committee of. the County Council Item this Parish Council, complaining of the foul smell from the Snip Canal and the old river bed at Wilderspool, and urging that steps be taken to ascertain to whom the responsibility attaches and whether any remedial measures can be taken to obviate the nuisance, which may involve very serious. con!ccuiences to the public health, and is already causing sickness as well as much discomfort and annoyance through the district which it affects." —Mr. Smith alluded to the fact that dead. bodies found in the canal at Stockton Heath were brought to Cirap. i penhall and pulled out of the water n the district. Bodies found in the canal were put in a boat and landed at the looks.—Mr. Boardman: They are found in one parish and brought into another.—The Clerk said that this was a matter which had been discussed before. It was a public matter. He spoke to the coroner at the last inquest about the matter,and the latter " coolly shut him up" by showing that what he said would not affect the verdict. That was all he could get out of him. (Laughter.)—Mr. Warburton observed that the bodies were brought to the locks because of the difficulty of landing them elsewhere in consequence of the steep embankment. —lt was reported that no steps had been taken to light Latchford Locks and to protect the public while passing over them.—lt was decided to instruct the Clerk to write to the responsible authorities again com- plaining that tbe matters referred to had not received attention.

STOCKTON HEATEI.

A meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council was held on Tuesday evening in St. Thomaas Scheo Stockton Heath. There were present—lVlessri. J. Whit- low (in the chair), J. Warburton, C. W. Davenport, Atifiton Hayes, F. Pearson, A. Broady, W. Copoock and J. Ellison (clerk). The Clerk read the following letter which had been received from Mr. Piers Egerton War- burton :—" April 23, 11398, Arley Hall, Northwich, Cheshire. Sir,—Before removing the fence between London Bridge and the Mulberry Tree I think it would be more satisfactory to both of us to get the settlement of this question as agreed to by your Parish Council and confirmed by the County Council. PGrhaps you will kindly get this done. I will then remove the existing fence as far as the gardens, leaving you to get the remaining Portion as per agreement. —Mr. Davenport: I don't think the County Council have anything to do with us taking the land.—The Chairman: They will have something to say about making the fory-Inth._mr. Davenport then proposed that the ,enty Coui‘cil be written to asking them to make the f, 'path, and that a reply from the Council be obtained before further action was taken in the matter.— ',qr. Warburton seconded the motion, and it was carried unanimously.—Mr. Pearson said there was an evident desire on the part of Mr. Warburton ty) meet the wishes of the Council. —Mr. Pearson : I be,r to give notice that I shall bring forward a motion for the planting of trees in the lanes and roads in the parish i .—The Clerk : I will see that tlc entered on the agenda.—This was all the business, and the meet- ing concluded.

It will be remembered that when the Warrington team visited Bootle a short time ago they returned home de- feated,perhaps not so much on their merits but more on account of the wretched state of the ground. The de- feat was the more distasteful to the Warrington team in-as-much as it was the first cheek in their victorioui career. On Saturday they were given an opportunity of avenging that defeat,and they did so in a very decided fashion. The result read as follows :—Bootle 83, W ir- rington 177. Credit must be chiefly given to Connor for this result who carried out his bat for a brilliantly com- piled 104.

The visitors were the first to go to the wickets and they commenced in a promising Manner. The initial batsmen hit out briskly and the result was that after ten minutes play, +26 runs were recorded. Connor now effected a separation, by sending Price an extra fast one which disturbed his wickets. Scrimshaw was also successful in his next over in bowling Clark with a fast " yorker," and he followed up his success in a determined manner. The succeeding five overs dismissed a man in each over, and the result was that eight wickets were down for the poor total of 51. On the partnership of Crouch and Johnson, the score was increased to 67 before a fine catch at cover point by Podmore. sent Crouch back to the pavilion. The Rev. H. E. Wilson succeeded him and proved a useful partner. His defensive play enabled Johnson to make what score he could. An accident oc- curred of this point of the game, for in stopping a hard cut by Johnson, Mr. J. J. Crosfield was &merely hurt on the hand. He retired from the field, and took no fur- ther part in the match. The visitors' innings was brought to a close by Scrimshaw accepting a smart return from Johnson. The fielding of the Warrington men was very fine, and !lampoon again proved himself a compatent wicket-keeper, only one bye being allowed by him. Scrimshaw was the most successful bowler, arid had the fine average of seven wickets for 34 runs.

The home team made a very unpromising start in their task of compiling 83 runs. Before any score had been made, Thewlis was bowled, and Bibby was run out when the runs made only numbered eleven. .1. Callon and Podmore were instrumental in increaling the score to 29, but when it bad reached that figure, Callon fell a victim to a good length ball from Price. H. Connor next joined Podmore, and had only made three when he was missed by Spence at short slip, and as after- wards proved this was the turning point of the match. Connor and Podmore both played very caut- iously and as a result they were able to practically de- cide the engagement. Despite changes in the bowling the score was increased to over 70 before Podmore was caught. The retiring batsman's contribution to the score was 23, and it was made when runs were most needed. One ball sufficed to dismiss Clarke, the home captain, who was succeeded by G. Crosfield, who made his debut on the Arpley ground. He remained with Connor until the Bootle score hid been passed, when Jones caught him behind the wicket. Nelson ' had only scored three when he was bowled and made way for Hamnson Connor continued to bat in first-class style, .his hitting all round being brilliant. Four succeeded four in quick succession, and on two occasions he almost drove the ball out of the enclosure. Hamnson continmed with him until he had scored 88, and Scrimshaw succeeded ic keeping his end up while Connor reached three figures. In one over the amateur scored ten for Browdbill and in Price's next over he hit three twos. When Connor's century was hoisted there was enthusiastic cheering.and this was renewed when shortly afterwards he-carried out his bat for 104.

Unlike their seniors the Warrington second eleven were unable to record a victory on Saturday's nritch with Bootle. They were away from horse and in fair- ness it may be said that they acquitted themselves creditably in making a draw against a total of 195. Homesters and visitors alike made a good score and were well represented in batting. Further similarity was that the first half-dozen wickets of each team realize,d a great preponderance of the runs.

Bootle opened their 1001ng4 with Jones and Parr. The former retired with 11 but his partner contributed 27 before placing the ball within Silcock's reach. Mains followed with 15 and Dr. M'L.ary macre a grand stan.i, carrying out his bat for 81. With him Gilmore made 23 and Smith 14 and the innings closed for 195 for 10 wickets. The visitors made an equally promising start, for England compiled 20, Evans 11 and Silcock 17, in succession. Then Unsworth made a" duck" and Bowen went on batting with vigour. He withstood all bowling and outlasted the match with 41. to his credit. Had he been blessed with a good batting partner near the end there would have been a possibility of passing the hlin total, but the batsmen who joined- him retired for sin a totals and time was called with Carter and Bowen n o out. 1.34 runs for 9 wickets is not by any means bad.

Another couple of fixtures were playel in the War- rington League on Saturday and again attracted a large amount of interest. The most momentous of the two was that between the two Sankey rivals :—The Wire Mills and Wesleyans.

Last week I commented uron the success of both team's and expressed the opinbn that prhaos the latter eleven would be able to put a spoke in the wheel of the Wire Mills, not recollecting that they would so soon have an opportunity. Their chanct, came on Saturday, but either the "spoke" was not thick enough,or the" wheel " ,vas too strong, at any rate it was not stayed in it. 4 vic- torious career. The match was played on the Wesleyans' ground and the visitors were the first to utilise the wicket. Williams and Bailey opened steadily but the former was run out at 9 and the latter caught for 11. Flelsby retired first ball and Cros4kill put 10 to the total. Later on Marshall contributed 16, and Woodcock and Simpson made a strorg partnership. Roth were run out, the former at 24, and the latter 22. Another 11 came from the bat of Warburton, and the innings was finished for 113. A poor start was made by the homes- tors two wickets being down for 28,but a valuable stand was made by Roderick and lip ;,)it. They had carried the score to 60 when Upson fell a victim to Hobson, for 22. Roderick maintained his positin, hut the other batsmen were unable to back him up and eventually he carried out his bat for 28: The innings realized 84. Hob- son's bowling was one of th features of the game he having t,ken 6 wickets for 21 runs. Marshall and War- burton also trundled well.

The Cheshire Lines Engineers opened their League fixture list by playing the Y.M.C. A. on Saturday at Battersby-lane. They did not make a high score but it brought them victory,the Y.M.C. A. again being victims. For the homesters Lawless made 22 runs, James Monks 16, and Brown 10. Barber was the only one who could score from the bowling against his side, an he batted very freely for his 21. James Monks and flarri,ion were again destructive with the ball, causing a succession of kalf-a-dozen"ducks"among the "young men," who were all out for 32. What has become of their old prowess ?

Good bowling again broo2ht success to the Brewery first eleven. They had as visitors the Weston team whom they dismissed from a round drzan. Todd and Allen were well nigh unplayable with the ball. Other- wise the team might have been beaten for they were unable to make a big score themsclves, all being out for 44.

The Weston defeat was handsomely atoned for by the second team of the same name, for they wiped out the Brewery second to the tune of 101 to 33. Tudor, Leach, and Williams were the best batsmen for the homesters. whose victory was also largely assisted by the bad wicket keeping of the visitors, who allowed 18 extras, and received none from Weston. Tudor and Pye were the men who made a hole in the batting averages of the P. W. and Son's eleven.

One of the most surprising games on Saturday was that between Buckley-st. and St. Elphins second. The Saints batted—or rather atterrpted to—first, and Jolley and Leach had them all out for 12. Victory wag then almost assured for Buckley-st., but they were appa- rently equal to much greater things. Jolley was run out for a "duck." but the only other two players who handled the bat were Spruce and Pugh, who made 94 between them and were not out. The former scored 50 and the latter 44, while 9 extras brought the total to 103. To record a victory of 9 wickets and 91 runs should be a feather in the Buckley-st. cap for some time to come.

St. Elphins first managed to defeat Winwick on Sat- urday by 14 runs. The game was of a rather close character, the scores being low :—St. Elphins 48, Win- wick 34. This success relieves the monotony of losses which have fallen to the Saints of late.

Padgate Wesleyans and a team representiag Messrs. Thewlis and Griffiths had a match at Padgate on Satur- day. Scoring was remarkably we4k, for the home team only compiled 43 runs, 26 of which were from Ashall's bat, and 7 extras. The visitors made a far worse display for their total was 11—a magnificeot avefage of 1 each. Davison and Ashall prevented a better performance by their excellent bowling. The latter took 5 wickets for four runs and performed the hat trick, and the former four wickets for seven runs. The Sankey Wire Mills and Penketh Wesleyans second teams made a closer thing of it than their seniors though victory lay with the same team 59 and 49 were thei r respective scores and the first batsman in each eleven made the best performance viz :—Cook for ' the Wire Mills 19, and Morrall for the Weslevans 17. Ellis and flighley also batted well for the respective elevens.

CHILD NEGLECT BY A FATHER.

DRINK AT THE BOTTOM OF IT.

On Thursday morning, before Mr. S. Chandley (chair- man) and Alderman Sutton, at the Warrington Borough Police Court, William O'Connof, 16. Cockhedge-lane.was charged with neglecting his four children on the 23rd May and during the last six months, in a manner likely to cause them suffering. Inspector McAteer, N.S.P.C.C.,said the defendant was a cabinet maker, and the father of six.children ranging in age from 18 to one year and nine months. The four children he was charged with neglecting were Mary age 9, Nora 7, John 4, and Eveline one year and nine months. On the 23rd of last month in consequence of a complaint he went in company with P.C. Wood to defendant's house, and found both doors open and no one there. The place was almost destitute. They could not find the children, but from further information they went to St. Mary's school and found them there. They examined them and found them in a filthy and deplor- able condition, insufficiently clothed, and dirty. De- fendant's wife was employed at the "Old Crow," and he would prove to them that defendant was a lazy, drunken man, and one who for the last six months had never allowed a penny to support his wife or the chil- dren. Defendant was seldom sober. On a certain night he went to the "Old Crow" and tried to get from his wife the money she had earned, but failed. He beat her and knocked one of her teeth out. It was 18 months since he (the Inspector) first visited the defendant, and the condition of his children had since gone worse. They were systematically neglected. Defendant: Have you ever seen -ne drunk. The Inspector: I have seen you drunk many times and no later than last Saturday night. P.C. Wood supp,,rtcd the Inspector's statement about examining the children on the 23rd May. They were filthy and had vermin on their heads and had hardly any boots on.

- Detective Meacnck said be heard a row at midnight on the 30th May in James-st., and going there found de- fendant drunk and standing at his house door with his wife and ens child in the street. He refused to admit them and was using_ threats.

Mrs. O'Connor said she had been employed at th "Old Crow" six months. During that time defendan had only given her 63., and that was since the Insp,ctor called. About two mouths ago a lady gave her some dress material for her children, and when defendaht Came home he pawned it and drank the mom.y. The neighbours had sometims to give the children food. Defendant was a drunken man. It was true that he broke one of her teeth when she refused him money. Mrs. Cranshaw, landlady of the" Old Crow," corro- borated as to the latter incident. She had herself given the children food. Defeniant said he had not the slightest hesitation in saying that there had been neglect, and he had been the cause of it,but not wilfully. He had never attempted to neglect his family. He was a well inclined man and honest, and was not either a blackguard or a lazy man.

The Chief Constable : He has been up 16 times for various offences and had a month's imprisonment for wife assault.

The magistrates sent dafenctint to gaol for three try.ntlis hard labour and the Chairman advised him to turn over a new leaf.

EBENEZER LODGE OF GOOD TEMPLARS.

On Saturday the members of this lodge had their annual picnic to Frodsham. The party numbering 28 left Earlestown in a waggonette at 1.30, but be- fore leaving Newton the conveyance broke down, which caused considerable delay,and it was 5 o'clock before they reached Frodsham. On arrival the mem- bers dispersed and rambled about the hills,enjoying the limited time at their disposal. Messrs. Taylor and Morris carried out the arrangements.

EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN PICNIC.

On Saturday the committee of the bazaar re- cently held in connection with the Earlestown Wesleyan Church treated the children and others who had worked during the days of the bazaar to an outing to Frodstiam. About 60 children and adults left Earlestown on Saturday at 11.30, reach- ing Frodsham shortly before one o'clock. They at once proceeded to Mersey View,where refreshments were partaken of. Afterwards the children dispersed, and rambling over the hills became the order until tea time, when all assembled at Mersey View and enjoyed a substantial tea. A :4-art for home was made about eight o'clock,Earlestown being reached about 9.3o,after a very pleasant time. The arrange- ments were in the hands of a committee, with Mr. J. R. Barr as secretary.

ACQUISITIVE FARNI LABOURERS AT EARLESTOWN. Ar, Hie Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Thomas Newton was brought up on remand from Earlestown, charged with stealing from a farm building in Newton, between the 28th and 29t ti inst., a pair of lace-up boots value 58., the property of Bernard Kennedy.. lie pleaded not guilt y.—Bernard Kennedy, working for Mr. Pen- nington, Farm-lane, Newton, said that on Sunday when he went to bed he left his boots on the weigh- ing machine. He got up about 8 o'clock and missed them. A pair of clogs was left in their place. He had seen defendant about on Thursday night, but did not suspect him then. On Monday he was in the Earlestown Arms and there found defendant wearing his boots. He took him to the Police Station and gave him in charge.—P.C. Turner said be charged the defendant who said "all right."—be was sent to gaol for a month's bard labour.—Patrick Moore, an Vish labourer, was charged with stealing a pair ot lace-up boots, the property of Thomas Kaye, trom Highfield Farm, Middleton, on the 29th June.—::e admitted the offence.—Thomas Kaye, labourer at Highfield Farm, said that he missed the boots on Monday at 6 o'clock. Prisoner worked at the same farm and when witness went out in the morning he left prisoner in the stable. When he mis.el the boots witness informed the police.— Joseph Ashton, assistant at Morton's pawnshop, Winwick-street, Warrington, said the prisoner offered the boots in pawn on the 29th, saying they were his own.—Prisoner was 'committed for a month.

WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB. On Saturday last about 30 members of the above club had a most enjoyable run to Winnington Sports. The return journey was made via Knuts- ford after a pleasant afternoon's sport. On Tuesday the club run was to Knutsford, but owing to the unsettled state of the weather, only a moderate number turned out. This week-end several tours will be in progress. One section travels to the Lake District, another through North Wales, while a third parry contemplates touring through the Nor- folk Br oads.Given fine weather some very enjoyable times may be expected.

On Tuesday afternoon Mr. S. Brighouse (county coroner), conducted an enquiry at the Police Court Irlam -street, Warrington, relative to the thath of James Harran, age 9. son of Jam's Barran, Newton-le-Wil- lows, who was supposed to have died from the effects of swallowing a pin in some bread. Mr. H. J. Longton, solicitor, was present representing Mr. MAyneux, who supplied the lad's father with bread, and Dr. Lingdale, of the Warrington Infirmary, also attended to relate the result of a post-mortem examination he had made upon the deceased.

• James Herren, senr., said he was a ticket collector,at Newton-le-Willows, in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Company, and lived at 102, Mersey-at., Newton-le-Willows. The deceased was bis son, and was nine years of age. Witness stated that he left for work about twenty-five minutes to seven on the 19th June, and he had to be at the station at a quarter to seven to meet the trains arriving at that time. The deceased was in bed at the time he left the house Shortly afterwards, when he came home, he was informed that his son had swallowed a pin. It seemed his son came to his breakfast, and began to eat some bread and bacon. When he had been eating tile bread some time, de?eased shouted to his sister Elizabeth, who was near by, that be had swallowed a pin. He was laid down on the rug close to the table' alid witness's daughter. Elizabeth, tapped him on the back, trying to fore it out. The daughter shouted to the mother, who immediately came downstairs, and shortly 'afterwateis the deceased was taken to Dr. Harold Watkins, Newton-le-Willows. Wit- ness explained to Dr. Watkins what had occurred, and also told him that he supposed his son had swallowed a a pin. The boy said at the time that it was a pin he had swallowed. The doctor tried to get the obstruction out of the throat, and, in fact, found nothing except a scratch on the inside of the neck. The doctor. supplied medicine, and attended the child till Friaay, when he got a little worse, and on the Saturday he was informed again . that deceased was going to choke and that something must be done. On the Saturday they treated him for abscess, pout- tieing him with linseed,and something which the doctor had given had to be put on the abscess. On the Sunday morning, witness said to his wife "The child is going to be choked." No milk could he got doa n deceased's throat. Witness went across to Dr. Watkins and told him he must come then. The doctor happened to be out when he called, and he left word with the servant that he must come to witness's residence at once. Later on, witness ascertained that Dr. Harold Watkins had been in Newton attending a case and witness went and told Dr. Watkins that his child was choking and he wanted some thing to make him better. He also told Dr. Watkins that he must do something. On the Sunday witness went to church, and Dr. Watkins had called and bad seen the boy and had then stated that he must be taken to Warrington some way or other, and if they could not take him by traim they must take him by cab. He again called at Dr. Watkins', when he was told that he was not in, and that he was attending a confinement case which was of a serious nature. Dr. Watkins told Witness that Ihe wanted to see him, and witness waited in the street lat Newton, opposite Dr. Watkins' house, from eleven till two o'cicck. His son was almost choking about that time. He was expecting Dr. Harold Watkins would come out and tell witness what to do. In fact, be was almost frantic.

The Coroner: How did you. eveatually bring the boy here ?—Witness: I brought him in a cib. Continuing, witness said Dr. Harold Watkins came and saw the child, and informed witness that be must take it to War- rington in a cab. Witness ran to Mr. Hewitt's and ordered a cab at about a varter-past two. Witness came with boy and was pre.ent with him when he died. When the cab arrived in Winwick-street, the deceased got up on his side and said "I am dying, I am choked." He got to the Infirmary about half-past three on the 28th inst.

The Coroner : When did he begin to take any pills ? —Witness: He did not take any pills till about a week after.

_ Coroner: Can you tell us about the time? I have a reason for asking, because we believe there were three pills in the larynx P—Witness : Oa the 27th June. Dr. Henry Langdale, senior housa surgeon at the War- rington Infirmary,stated that the deceased was admitted to the Warrington Infirmary on Sunday last, and was then in a dying condition, and died shortly after admis- sion. By the coroner's order he had made an examina- tion of the body, and found nothing noteworthy exter- nally, except the operation mark which witness had made. At the back of the throat there was an abscess, which contained several ounces of matter. In the abscess cavity there was a small piece of wire and three pills had got in the throat. One 01 these pills had got embedded in the larynx. The piece of wire was at the back of the abscess.

The Coroner : What I what to get at is this. Suppose the pills were taken, we have no evhlence of the exact time the swallowing of the wire took place. If it was done on Saturday last and the pills were taken on the 27th inst., would there be anything to guide you as to What his condition would bp on the previous clay? Mr. Langdale: The change in the child's condition may have been much more rapid that I could say. A Juryman From what has been said 1 do not know how the doctor could give a child pills with a throat like that. • The Coroner: That is what I want to ascertain-- whether the child was in such a condition as to take the Dr. Langdale : I gathered from the history of the case that Dr. Watkins had examined the throat as well as possible, and came to the conclusion that his treatment was desirable. The abscess in the child's throat was not of long standing ; the pin might have been long embed- ded.

Tile Coroner : A medical man knowing before that the throat was in such a condition, the swallowing of pills would be dangerous. Dr. Langdale: The examination of a child's throt is always a difficult one. The Coroner: Would yod give pills ? Dr. Langdale : Looking at it after, I don't think I should, but before I dovet know. A Juryman expre:.sA a desire " to get to the bottom of the the thing." There were sufficient sorts of medicine to give children without giving pills. Mr. Longton : Of course you don't know how that piece of wire got into the child's throat, whether it was by means of the bread or otherwise? Dr. Langdale: No. The Coroner What is the opinion of the jury ? What do you say, Mr. Foreman? The Foreman : I think it is unanimous that Dr. Wat- kins should be here, The Coroner : It is against all our ideas of fairness to make allegations against a doctor when he is not here.

The inquiry was then adjourned to the 17th July.

INQUEST AT WARRINGTON. SINGULAR EVIDENCE.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL 'CORRESPONDENT.

The District Council have at last seen their way to run dry the pond at the top of Market-st., and thus rem ova all source of danger to daring young- sters, and set at rest the minds of anxious parents. I am sure the residents in the neighbourhood of the pit will be as eager to thank the Council as they were to petition them for the removal of the nui- sance. There are other ponds in the district equally as useless and quite as dangerous as the one in ques- tion. If they will turn their attention to Wargrave- road they will find two or three which could very well be spared. In two of them children have been drowned, and only last week a young lady risked her life to rescue a child which had fallen into the water. I would recommend the Council to consider the desirability of having these emptied also.

The meeting held last week in connection with the proposed Gilchrist lectures was only very poorly attended. No doubt, as the Chairman said, the balmy weather had something to do with that, because workingmen as a rule do not care after a day's hard work to he cooped up in a room to listen to dry speeches. This, however, was a very inter- esting meeting, and being upon a very important subject concerning working men, I think a better attendance should have been assured. Still the object for which it was called, was attained, although seeing that the lectures are specially designed for the working classes, I think a greater effort might have been made to have the committee which was appointed, more largely composed of the working classes, pure and simple. Perhaps. how- ever, as the proviso "with power to add to their number" was inserted, those elected may see their way to invite several working men to join them.

--- The May Queen Festival Committee have been a little smarter this year in issuing a statement of their finances. Last, year several months elapsed before a balance sheet was issued, but the grumbl- ing which was then occasioned has evidently been beneficial. I notice the balance on this year's. festival comes a few coppers short of a sovereign Many people may wonder why the profit is so small but a comparison between this and last year's state- ments will show them that the takings at the gates were something like £9 less than last year. More expense was also incurred in the engagement of an extra band, and upon attractions at the coronation ground. On the whole, I think the Committee have done remarkably well, and I hope on another occa- ion they may fu rthei improve their position.

RAILWAY SPORTS.—At the Sale Petty Sessions, on Monday, Mr. William George Mel- bourne, hotel keeper, of Grasgendale, near Liverpool,ap- plied for permission to sell at Mr. John Leigh's field, Lymm,from twelve to eight on the occasion of the Lon- don and North-Western Railway sports.—Mr. George Rooke, one of the mag,istratesiobserved that hardly time enough had been given for the police to make inquiries. _The application was granted, subject to no objection being raised by the police. PADGATE. __— ROYAL LANCASHIRE AGRICULTURAL SnCIETY.— At a well-attended meeting of the Council of the Royal Lan- cashire Agricultural Society, held on the Show ground, Southport, on Tuesday. Mr. Walter Bennett, ef Padgate, was elected one of the life members. Two gamekeepers surprised a party of poachers in Grove Park,near Warwick,early on Wednesday morning. Three of the poachers ran away,but the fourth remained and in a struggle which ensued with one of the game- keepers he was shot through the head with a revolver. An inquest was held on Wednesday, at which it was stated that the revolver accidentally went off during a tussle between the two men, anyl a verdict of " Acciden- tal death " was returned.
